Foundations for Deductive Verification of Continuous Probabilistic Programs Note: This document is the full version of a paper with the same title published at OOPSLA 2025.
Kevin Batz, Joost-Pieter Katoen, Francesca Randone, Tobias Winkler

Abstract
. We lay out novel foundations for the computer-aided verification of guaranteed bounds on expected outcomes of imperative probabilistic programs featuring (i) general loops , (ii) continuous distributions, and (iii) conditioning . To handle loops we rely on user-provided quantitative invariants , as is well established. However, in the realm of continuous distributions, invariant verification becomes extremely challenging due to the presence of integrals in expectation-based program semantics. Our key idea is to soundly under- or over-approximate these integrals via Riemann sums . We show that this approach enables the SMT-based invariant verification for programs with a fairly general control flow structure. On the theoretical side, we prove convergence of our Riemann approximations, and establish 𝖼𝗈𝖱𝖤 \mathsf{coRE} -completeness of the central verification problems. On the practical side, we show that our approach enables to use existing automated verifiers targeting discrete probabilistic programs for the verification of programs involving continuous sampling . Towards this end, we implement our approach in the recent quantitative verification infrastructure Caesar by encoding Riemann sums in its intermediate verification language. We present several promising case studies.

Theory of computation Probabilistic computation
CCS:

From Lebesgue to Riemann and Back

1.
Introduction

Probabilistic programs (PP) are usual programs with the additional ability to

(1)

branch
their flow of control based on the outcomes of coin flips,

(2)

sample
numbers from predefined continuous and/or discrete probability distributions, and

(3)

condition
their current state on observations using dedicated
\mathtt{observe}
-instructions.

Programs with one or more of these (partly overlapping) abilities have been extensively studied in different contexts and throughout various communities:

Randomized algorithms
typically rely on ability (
1
) to speed up computations on average
(
Karp, 1991
)
or for breaking symmetries in distributed computing
(
Itai and Rodeh, 1990
)
.

Sampling
and
conditioning
(abilities (
2
) and (
3
)) are extensively employed in connection with Bayes’ rule in machine learning, AI, and cognitive science to statistically infer information from observed data
(
Goodman and Stuhlmüller, 2014
;
Gordon et al., 2014
;
van de Meent et al., 2018
)
.

Continuous distributions
are pivotal for these applications.

Many applications require
expressive
PP with the usual constructs from traditional programs — including unbounded
\mathtt{while}
-loops.
The latter pose a major challenge:
For instance, reasoning about probabilistic termination is provably harder than reasoning about classical termination
(
Kaminski and Katoen, 2017
)
.

Guaranteed Bounds on Expected Outcomes via Weakest Pre-Expectations

In recent years, there has been considerable interest in analyzing expressive PP in a mathematically rigorous manner — with
hard
rather than statistical guarantees — see e.g.,
(
Chakarov and Sankaranarayanan, 2013
;
Gehr et al., 2016
;
Agrawal et al., 2018
;
Moosbrugger et al., 2022b
;
Gehr et al., 2020
)
as representative examples.
Such stringent analyses are motivated by the fact that more conventional statistical techniques can be far off even for simple programs
(
Beutner et al., 2022
)
, and may be inappropriate for safety-critical applications.

The
weakest pre-expectation (
\mathsf{wp}
) calculus
is a prominent means to specify and establish a broad spectrum of PP properties in a rigorous manner
(
Kozen, 1983
;
McIver and Morgan, 2005
;
Kaminski, 2019
)
.
Generalizing Dijkstra’s classical weakest pre-
conditions
, the central objects are so-called
expectations

f
— random variables over a program’s state space mapping program states to numbers — which take over the role of predicates from classical program verification.
More precisely, given a probabilistic program
C
, an expectation
f
, and an initial program state
\sigma
, we have

\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)(\sigma)\quad=\quad\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\normalsize\emph{expected value} of $f$ w.r.t.\ the distribution of \emph{final} states}\\
\text{\normalsize reached after executing $C$ on \emph{initial} state $\sigma$}\\
\text{\normalsize and aborting all executions violating an observation}~.\end{subarray}

Hence,
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)
is a map from
initial
program states to
expected final
values of
f
.
The weakest
liberal
pre-expectation
\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)
adds to the above quantity the probability of
C
’s divergence
1
1

1

Provided
f
is upper-bounded by
1
.
See
Section

4.2
for details.
.
Both
\mathsf{wp}
and
\mathsf{wlp}
can be defined by induction on the structure of
C
.
Notice that neither of these calculi yield
conditional
expected values as one might expect in the presence of conditioning.
One can, however, define
conditional weakest pre-expectations

(
Olmedo et al., 2018
;
Nori et al., 2014
)
as
2
2

2

Notice that this quantity is undefined if
\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(1\right)(\sigma)=0
as is natural when conditioning on a probability-
0
-event.

\mathsf{cwp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)(\sigma)~{}={}~\frac{\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)(\sigma)}{\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(1\right)(\sigma)}~{}={}~\frac{\text{\normalsize{expected value} of $f$ \ldots}}{\text{probability of not violating an observation}}~,

which indeed yields the sought-after conditional expected values.
We refer to quantities such as
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)(\sigma)
,
\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)(\sigma)
, and
\mathsf{cwp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)(\sigma)
as
(conditional) expected outcomes
of probabilistic programs.
Reasoning about expected outcomes of loops is typically tackled by means of
quantitative loop invariants
, which are, naturally, often hard to find.
However, in the presence of continuous sampling, even
verifying
a
given
candidate loop invariant poses severe challenges as reasoning about the required expected values involves possibly complex
integrals
.
The aim of this paper is to lay the foundations for automated techniques tackling these challenges.

Problem Statement

We study
imperative
PP with all three abilities (
1
), (
2
), and (
3
).
Our goal is to

semi-automatically verify bounds on (conditional) expected outcomes of programs featuring
continuous uni
f
orm sam
p
lin
g, unbounded
\mathtt{while}
-loo
p
s
with user-provided q
uantitative invariants
, and
conditionin
g.

“Semi-automatic”
means that we focus on verification of
user-provided
quantitative loop invariants in the sense of
(
McIver and Morgan, 2005
)
.
Unlike fully automatic approaches we do not
synthesize
such invariants automatically, which is a promising direction for future work but outside the scope of this paper.

Approach

Our key idea is simple, yet powerful:

We replace the
inte
g
rals
occurring in the definition of the programs’ exact weakest pre-expectations by simpler
sound a
pp
roximations
, namely lower and upper
Riemann sums
.

This results in a family of approximate
lower
and
upper Riemann
\mathsf{wp}
transformers
, denoted by
\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N}
and
\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}
, where
N
is the number of sub-intervals used to discretize the domain of integration.
Crucially, these Riemann expectation transformers ultimately give rise to automated SMT-based techniques for verifying quantitative loop invariants.
Let us now consider an introductory example.

Illustrative Example: A Monte Carlo Approximator.

The program shown in
Figure

1
draws
\mathtt{M}

(x,y)
-samples uniformly at random from the unit square.
Each time a sample lands in the quarter unit circle, the variable
\mathtt{count}
is incremented.
The program hence approximates the transcendental number
\tfrac{\pi}{4}\approx 0.785
(the area of the quarter unit circle) in a Monte Carlo manner.

We will first focus on the
blue
fragment
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3281,0.625}C_{inner}}
of the program from
Figure

1
.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.3281,0.625}C_{inner}}
contains two uniform continuous sampling instructions.
Suppose we aim to compute the expected value of
\mathtt{count}
after executing
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3281,0.625}C_{inner}}
.
Applying the rules for determining
\mathsf{wp}
’s
(
Szymczak and Katoen, 2019
)
, we obtain the following double Lebesgue integral, where
{\left[\ldots\right]}
denotes the indicator function of the enclosed predicate:

(
\dagger
)

\displaystyle\mathsf{wp}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.3281,0.625}C_{inner}}\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{count}\right)~{}={}~\underbrace{\mathtt{count}+\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}{\left[\mathtt{x}^{2}+\mathtt{y}^{2}\leq 1\right]}\,d\bm{\lambda}(x),d\bm{\lambda}(y)~{}={}~\mathtt{count}+\frac{\pi}{4}}_{\text{expected final value of $\mathtt{count}$ in terms of its initial value}}~.

This reflects the fact that, in
expectation
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3281,0.625}C_{inner}}
increments
\mathtt{count}
by
\tfrac{\pi}{4}
.

Symbolic integration-based tools such as PSI
(
Gehr et al., 2016
)
can solve the above integral directly.
However, we observe two significant issues:
(i) there are integrals that cannot be evaluated symbolically in closed form,
and (ii) even if all guards and assignments in the program are polynomial and distributions are restricted to uniform distributions in
[0,1]
, the resulting integrals may evaluate to transcendental numbers, making automation notoriously difficult.

As outlined, instead of solving integrals exactly, our strategy is to soundly
under-
or
over-approximate
them by lower or upper Riemann sums.
In our example, the resulting upper sum

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}~\sup_{\xi\in[\frac{i}{N},\frac{i+1}{N}]}~\sup_{\zeta\in[\frac{j}{N},\frac{j+1}{N}]}~{\left[\xi^{2}+\zeta^{2}\leq 1\right]}~,

where
N\geq 1
, is a guaranteed upper bound on the double integral in (

†

‣
1
).
For instance, with
N=16
, we can verify that the upper sum is at most
0.85
, and hence that
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.3281,0.625}C_{inner}}\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{count}\right)(\sigma)\leq\sigma(\mathtt{count})+0.85
for all initial states
\sigma
.
Crucially, to prove upper bounds on upper Riemann sums, we do
not
have to evaluate any suprema explicitly because for all
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
,

\sup A\leq b\qquad\text{iff}\qquad\text{$\forall a\in A$}\colon a\leq b~.

In practice, we can thus drop the
\sup
’s in upper Riemann sums (and, dually, the
\inf
’s in lower sums) by introducing
\forall
-quantifiers, which is highly beneficial in the context of SMT-based automation.

We stress that even though we discretize the integrals’ domains uniformly, our approach is
not
the same as replacing the continuous
\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}
distributions by discrete uniform distributions of
N
point-masses.
Indeed, the latter would neither yield under- nor over-approximations in general, which is, however, essential for invariant verification.

\displaystyle\mathtt{i}\coloneq 1\,;\,\mathtt{count}\coloneq 0\,;\,

\displaystyle\mathtt{while}~(\mathtt{i}\leq\mathtt{M})~\{

\displaystyle\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0.3281,0.625}\mathtt{x}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}\,;\,\mathtt{y}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}\,;}

\displaystyle\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0.3281,0.625}\mathtt{if}(\mathtt{x}^{2}+\mathtt{y}^{2}\leq 1)~\{\mathtt{count}\coloneq\mathtt{count}+1\}~\mathtt{else}~\{\mathtt{skip}\}\,;}

\displaystyle\qquad\mathtt{i}\coloneq\mathtt{i}+1\quad\}

0.25
0.5
0.75
1
0.25
0.5
0.75
1
x
y

Figure 1
.

Left: Monte-Carlo approximator
C
for
\pi
using ability (
2
).
Right: 100 random samples.

Loops and Invariants

Now, consider the entire program
C
in
Figure

1
.
We have
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{count}\right)=\tfrac{\pi}{4}\cdot\mathtt{M}
because, intuitively, the percentage of samples landing in the quarter circle equals its area.
Our techniques enable to soundly bound
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{count}\right)
in a semi-automated manner
for arbitrary initial values of
\mathtt{M}
.
This is done, as is standard in deductive verification, using quantitative loop invariants, which we detail in
Section

6
.
Suffices to say here that, given a suitable invariant
I
, our techniques enable to verify its validity automatically.
This in turn allows us to conclude that:

\forall\,\text{initial states $\sigma$}\colon\quad\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{count}\right)(\sigma)~{}\leq{}~0.85\cdot\sigma(\mathtt{M})~.

See
Example

6.3
(
example

6.3
) and
Example

9.1
(
example

9.1
) for details.

Contributions

In summary, this paper introduces the novel
lower
and
upper Riemann weakest pre-expectation transformers
and demonstrates their applicability to the semi-automated verification of PP with
continuous uniform sampling
and general
\mathtt{while}
-loops
.
Put more concretely:

Verification of Invariants:

We show that externally provided loop invariant candidates can be verified using the Riemann
\mathsf{wp}
transformers in a way that is suitable for SMT-based automation.
Such invariants, once verified, imply (one-sided) bounds on
\mathsf{wp}
,
\mathsf{wlp}
, and
\mathsf{cwp}
.

Convergence and Complexity:

On the more theoretical side, our approximate Riemann
\mathsf{wp}
’s are shown to
converge
to the exact
\mathsf{wp}
’s under mild assumptions as the discretization becomes finer.
As a consequence, we obtain
\mathsf{coRE}
-completeness
3
3

3

This means that the refutation of such bounds is semi-decidable.
results for the problems of verifying upper bounds on
\mathsf{wp}
and lower bounds on
\mathsf{wlp}
for fairly general loopy programs.

Implementation and Case Studies:

We incorporate our method in the PP verification infrastructure
Caesar

(
Schröer et al., 2023
)
by encoding Riemann sums in its
intermediate verification language
.
This enables transferring principles from classical SMT-based program verification
(
Müller et al., 2016
)
, such as custom first-order theories for reasoning about exponentials, to the verification of PPs involving continuous sampling.
We provide various case studies and an empirical evaluation.

Paper Structure

2
provides a bird’s eye view on the distinctive features and technicalities of our method.

Section

3
introduces basic fixed point theory and
Section

4
summarizes the existing definitions of
\mathsf{wp}
,
\mathsf{wlp}
, and
\mathsf{cwp}
.
In
Section

5
, we introduce our Riemann
\mathsf{wp}
transformers and prove them sound.

Section

6
is devoted to loop invariants.
The convergence results are presented in
Section

7
.
Towards automation,
Section

8
introduces a concrete, effective syntax for expectations.

Section

9
details the implementation in
Caesar
and presents case studies.
Additional related work is surveyed in
Section

10
.
We conclude in
Section

11
.
Omitted proofs can be found in
Appendix

C
.

2.
A Bird’s Eye View

In this section, we explain what makes our method unique by providing a compressed overview of its characteristic features.
We also discuss its limitations and point out a subtle technical challenge.

2.1.
Highlights and Comparison to Other Approaches

Since we are not the first to address the problem of proving bounds on quantities expressible as
\mathsf{w}(\mathsf{l})\mathsf{p}
’s, we now highlight five distinctive features of our approach — their combination is unique in the literature.
A more in-depth review of related work is deferred to
Section

10
.

Of the following five items, the first two are specific to the way we handle integrals via Riemann sums, whereas the latter three tend to apply to
\mathsf{wp}
-based approaches in general.

An Alternative to Moment-Based Analyses

A major thread of existing work, e.g.,
(
Chakarov and Sankaranarayanan, 2014
;
Agrawal et al., 2018
;
Moosbrugger et al., 2022b
)
, circumvents explicit integration altogether.
They achieve this by focusing on classes of programs and properties where, simply speaking, the analysis can soundly replace the distributions in the program by their
mean

(
Agrawal et al., 2017
, Appendix A, p. 31)
or some higher moment
(
Moosbrugger et al., 2022b
)
.
For example,
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket\mathtt{x}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{x}\mathtt{y}\right)
is equal to
4
4

4

In general, replacing
\mathtt{x}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}
by
\mathtt{x}\coloneq\tfrac{1}{2}
is sound whenever the post-expectation is linear in
\mathtt{x}
. While our approach is compatible with such optimizations, we shall not discuss them any further.

\mathsf{wp}\llbracket\mathtt{x}\coloneq\tfrac{1}{2}\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{x}\mathtt{y}\right)=\tfrac{1}{2}y
, where
\tfrac{1}{2}
is the mean of
\mathtt{unif}{[0,1]}
.
However, such mean-based analyses do not always suffice.
Indeed, reconsidering the example from
Section

1
, the inequality
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.3281,0.625}C_{inner}}\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{count}\right)\sqsubseteq\mathtt{count}+0.85
, proved correct with our approach, becomes false if we substitute
\mathtt{x}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}
and
\mathtt{y}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3281,0.625}C_{inner}}
by
\mathtt{x}\coloneq\tfrac{1}{2}
and
\mathtt{y}\coloneq\tfrac{1}{2}
, respectively.
In fact, the so-obtained program
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3281,0.625}C_{inner}}^{\prime}
satisfies
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.3281,0.625}C_{inner}}^{\prime}\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{count}\right)=\mathtt{count}+1
as the point
(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})

is certainly inside
the quarter unit circle.

General Conditional Branching

We make relatively mild assumptions regarding the shape of
\mathtt{if}
- and
\mathtt{while}
-guards in the programs — in practice, general Boolean combinations of the polynomial (in)equalities over all program variables are supported, as in
Figure

1
.
This is different from, e.g.,
(
Moosbrugger et al., 2022b
)
which restricts to finitely-valued variables in guards or
(
Chakarov and Sankaranarayanan, 2014
;
Chatterjee et al., 2016b
)
which restrict to linear guards.
We achieve this high level of generality thanks to the simplicity of the Riemann approximation which treats integration over the resulting indicator functions in a uniform manner.

Moving Backwards: Verification and Parametric Models

We conduct a
backward analysis
:
Given a random variable
f
over final program states (called
post-expectation
in this paper), we approximate the (conditional) weakest pre-expectation
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)
, which is
a function of the initial state
.
This is dual to works like
(
Gehr et al., 2016
;
Beutner et al., 2022
;
Wang et al., 2024
)
that conduct a
forward analysis
to solve a classic Bayesian inference problem, namely to compute a program’s posterior density —
a function of the final state
— for a given prior distribution.
Let us contrast these two paradigms in greater detail.

Backward analysis, as done in this paper, allows verifying program properties that hold for
all initial states
.
For instance, our framework supports questions such as,
“
Is the posterior mean of
x
at most twice the initial value of
y
?
”
, expressed as
\mathsf{cwp}[C](x)\leq 2y
.
The backward approach is thus well-suited for tasks such as
verification
— ensuring a program behaves as intended
for all inputs
— and
parametric analysis
— examining how expected behavior changes when program parameters are altered (parameters can be modeled as uninitialized program variables).

Forward analyses, on the other hand, are typically motivated by probabilistic programming languages (PPLs) explicitly designed for automating Bayesian inference, see e.g.,
(
van de Meent et al., 2018
;
Goodman and Stuhlmüller, 2014
;
Bingham et al., 2019
;
Carpenter et al., 2017
)
.
We remark that there are some fundamental discrepancies between these PPLs and the one studied in this paper.
For example, the former usually offer extensive support for general continuous distributions, but do not always support general loops.
Moreover, Bayesian inference problems often require
soft conditioning
, which our approach only encodes as syntactic sugar (see Remark 2).
Finally, some applications of Bayesian inference involve loading and processing datasets with thousands of observations.
In principle, such data could be hardcoded into our programs, but the result would likely be too large for current SMT-based analysis techniques (as indicated by our experiments in
Section

9.3
).

In summary, our backward approach is particularly suited for proving properties of complex stochastic processes described as probabilistic programs, especially those involving unbounded loops
However, it is less effective for data-intensive statistical analysis.

A Zoo of Quantities

The majority of existing methods for (semi-)automated exact analysis of PP with loops and continuous distributions focus on one of these tasks:
(i) bound the posterior distribution
(
Gehr et al., 2016
;
Beutner et al., 2022
;
Wang et al., 2024
)
,
(ii) prove various flavors of (non-)termination, e.g.,
(
Chakarov and Sankaranarayanan, 2013
;
Chatterjee et al., 2016a
;
Agrawal et al., 2018
)
, and
(iii) bound assertion violation probabilities
(
Chakarov and Sankaranarayanan, 2013
;
Wang et al., 2021b
)
.
An exception is
(
Wang et al., 2019
)
which tackles
cost analysis
.
In contrast, in the
\mathsf{wp}
-based framework, we can express and reason about a remarkably large variety of quantities, including the following (assume that
C
does not contain conditioning for simplicity):

•

Termination probabilities:
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(1\right)

•

The probability of terminating in a predicate
5
5

5

Recall that
{\left[\varphi\right]}
is the indicator function of the predicate
\varphi
.

\varphi
:
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left({\left[\varphi\right]}\right)

•

The expected value of variable
\mathtt{x}
on termination:
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{x}\right)

•

Higher moments of
\mathtt{x}
after termination:
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{x}^{k}\right)
for
k\geq 1

•

Expected distance between variables
\mathtt{x}
and
\mathtt{y}
after termination:
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(|\mathtt{x}-\mathtt{y}|\right)

Note that this includes reasoning about
unbounded
random variables.
For example, a program variable
\mathtt{x}
may become arbitrarily large during program execution.

Example 2.1.

Let us illustrate the above quantities.
Consider the following program
C
:

\displaystyle\mathtt{if}(\mathtt{x}=1)~\{\{\mathtt{y}\coloneq 0\}~[\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}]~\{\mathtt{y}\coloneq 2\}\}~\mathtt{else}~\{\{\mathtt{y}\coloneq 0\}~[\nicefrac{{4}}{{5}}]~\{\mathtt{y}\coloneq 3\}\}

•

\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(1\right)=1
, i.e.,
C
terminates with probability
1
for each initial state.

•

\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left({\left[\mathtt{y}=0\right]}\right)~{}={}~\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot{\left[\mathtt{x}=1\right]}+\nicefrac{{4}}{{5}}\cdot{\left[\mathtt{x}\neq 1\right]}
, i.e., if initially
\mathtt{x}=1
, then the probability to terminate in
\mathtt{y}=0
is
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
. For all other initial states, this probability is
\nicefrac{{4}}{{5}}
.

•

\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{y}\right)=1\cdot{\left[\mathtt{x}=1\right]}+\nicefrac{{3}}{{5}}\cdot{\left[\mathtt{x}\neq 1\right]}
, i.e., the expected final value of
\mathtt{y}
is either
1
or
\nicefrac{{3}}{{5}}
, depending on whether
\mathtt{x}=1
holds initially or not.

•

\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{y}^{2}\right)=2\cdot{\left[\mathtt{x}=1\right]}+\nicefrac{{9}}{{5}}\cdot{\left[\mathtt{x}\neq 1\right]}
, i.e., the second moment of
\mathtt{y}
on termination is either
2
or
\nicefrac{{9}}{{5}}
, depending on whether
\mathtt{x}=1
holds initially or not.

•

\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(|\mathtt{x}-\mathtt{y}|\right)={\left[\mathtt{x}=1\right]}\cdot(\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot|\mathtt{x}|+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot|\mathtt{x}-2|)+{\left[\mathtt{x}\neq 1\right]}\cdot(\nicefrac{{4}}{{5}}\cdot|\mathtt{x}|+\nicefrac{{1}}{{5}}\cdot|\mathtt{x}-3|)
, which is the expected difference between the final values of
\mathtt{x}
and
\mathtt{y}
in terms of their initial values.

The above weakest pre-expectations can be determined by applying the rules in
Table

1
(
table

1
).

Conditioning, Everywhere

Following
(
Olmedo et al., 2018
)
, we allow conditioning in the form of (hard)
\mathtt{observe}
-statements at arbitrary places in the program — even inside loops.
All of the above quantities can be generalized sensibly to the conditional setting.
We can go further:
For instance, the probability to never violate an
\mathtt{observe}
, even if the program does not terminate, is given by
\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(1\right)
.

2.2.
Limitations and Assumptions

As mentioned, to analyze loops, we assume
user-provided invariants
.
Moreover, we restrict to native support for continuous
uniform
distributions and discrete coin flips.
Further, while we support two-sided bounds for loop-free programs, the kind of invariants we study in this paper can only prove
upper
bounds on
\mathsf{wp}
and
\mathsf{cwp}
, and
lower
bounds on
\mathsf{wlp}
.
We conjecture that our approach extends to invariant-based methods for the other directions
(
Hark et al., 2020
)
as well, but leave the details for future work.
To keep the presentation simple, we only discuss invariant verification for
non-nested loops
.
The case of general loop structures, including sequential and/or nested, can be dealt with as in
(
Batz et al., 2024
)
.
We assume non-negative real-valued program variables and do not support data structures.
Soft-conditioning is only supported indirectly.
See
Remarks

1
,
3
and
2
for more details.

2.3.
From Lebesgue Integrals to Riemann Sums and Back: A Technical Challenge

The Lebesgue integral is
the
standard integral in probability theory.
There is good reason for this:
Lebesgue integrals can be defined for a broader class of functions than, say, Riemann integrals, and have favorable mathematical properties like Monotone Convergence Theorems.

Therefore, to define the semantics of PPs, most works (e.g.,
(
Szymczak and Katoen, 2019
;
Dahlqvist et al., 2020
)
) have resorted to Lebesgue integrals, and we do not question that this is the way to go.
To illustrate the usefulness of the Lebesgue integral as opposed to the Riemann integral
6
6

6

The Riemann integral is the limit of both the lower and upper Riemann sums as
N\to\infty
, provided the two limits coincide.
, consider the following program
D
:

\displaystyle\mathtt{x}\coloneq 0\,;\,\mathtt{y}\coloneq 1\,;

\displaystyle\mathtt{while}~(\mathtt{y}\cdot\mathtt{z}\neq\mathtt{x})~\{\mathtt{y}\coloneq\mathtt{y}+1\,;\,\mathtt{x}\coloneq 0\,;\,\mathtt{while}~(\mathtt{y}\cdot\mathtt{z}\neq\mathtt{x}\land\mathtt{x}<\mathtt{y})~\{\mathtt{x}\coloneq\mathtt{x}+1\}\,\}\}

This program
“searches”
for non-negative integers
\mathtt{x}
and
\mathtt{y}
such that
\mathtt{z}=\tfrac{\mathtt{x}}{\mathtt{y}}
and stops once it finds such
\mathtt{x}
and
\mathtt{y}
.
It thus terminates if and only if
\mathtt{z}
is a
rational number
in
{{[0,1]}}
initially (recall that we allow actual
real
-valued variables).
In symbols, we have
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket D\rrbracket\left(1\right)={\left[\mathtt{z}\in\mathbb{Q}\land 0\leq\mathtt{z}\leq 1\right]}
— this is the well-known
Dirichlet function
restricted to the unit interval.
Now, consider the program
\mathtt{z}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}\,;\,D
.
Its termination probability is zero, the Lebesgue integral of the Dirichlet function over the interval
{{[0,1]}}
.
Intuitively, this is because almost all real numbers are irrational, i.e., the rational numbers have Lebesgue measure zero.
However, the Dirichlet function is
not Riemann-integrable
— it is
“too discontinuous”
.
As a consequence, one cannot easily define
\mathsf{wp}
— neither in general nor in this specific example — relying on Riemann integrals.

In light of the above example, the following question arises naturally:

How sensible is an analysis of loops based on Riemann sums given the fact that
\mathsf{wp}
’s of loops are
not Riemann-inte
g
rable
in general, not even for programs using only polynomial arithmetic and constant post-expectations?

This question has at least two dimensions.

(1) Regarding
soundness
, we prove that our Riemann
\mathsf{wp}
’s are
always
sound under- or over-approximations, i.e., the following inequalities hold for all
N\geq 1
in a very general setting:

\forall~\text{initial states $\sigma$}\colon\quad\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)(\sigma)~{}\leq{}~\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)(\sigma)~{}\leq{}~\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)(\sigma)

and similarly for
\mathsf{wlp}
.
This works because the
lower Riemann integral
— the limit of the lower Riemann sums as the discretization becomes fines — is a lower bound on the Lebesgue integral, and similarly for the upper Riemann integral.
This is always true, even if the lower and upper integral are different, i.e., if the function at hand is not Riemann-integrable.

(2) Will the Riemann approximation always converge (in some sense) to the exact
\mathsf{wp}
as the discretization becomes finer?
We prove that, under mild assumptions such as polynomial arithmetic in the program, the answer is
yes
for loop-free programs.
As one of our theoretical main results for loopy programs, we show that (
Theorem

7.6
)

\sup_{n\geq 1}\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{n}\llbracket C^{n}\rrbracket\left(f\right)~{}={}~\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)\qquad\text{but}\qquad\inf_{n\geq 1}\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{n}\llbracket C^{n}\rrbracket\left(f\right)~\overset{\text{in general}}{\neq}~\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)~,

where
C^{n}
arises from
C
by unfolding all loops up to depth
n
by taking a simultaneous limit of the unfolding depth and the fineness of the lower Riemann sum approximation (the
n
in
\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{n}
).
Via equation (
2.3
) we
recover
the exact
\mathsf{wp}
— defined in terms of
Lebesgue
integrals — as a limit of our approximate
\mathsf{wp}
’s based on lower Riemann sums for a general class of probabilistic loops.

3.
Preliminaries

In this section, we set up our notations and treat the fixed point-theoretic foundations we rely on.

3.1.
General Notation

\mathbb{N}
is the set of non-negative integers.
The set of non-negative
extended reals
is
{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\cup\{\infty\}
.
We adopt the following standard conventions:
For all
x\in{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
, we let
x\leq\infty
and
x+\infty=\infty+x=\infty
.
Moreover, we define
\infty\cdot 0=0\cdot\infty=0
and
x\cdot\infty=\infty\cdot x=\infty
for all
x>0
.
Given
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote by
{[a,b]}
the real closed interval with endpoints
a
and
b
.
The set of truth values is
\mathbb{B}=\{\mathsf{false},\mathsf{true}\}
.
Given a predicate
\varphi\colon A\to\mathbb{B}
over a set
A
, we define the
Iverson bracket

\displaystyle{\left[\varphi\right]}\colon A\to\{0,1\},~a\mapsto\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }\varphi(a)=\mathsf{false}~,\\
1&\text{ if }\varphi(a)=\mathsf{true}~.\end{cases}

For example, assuming that it is understood from context that
x
is a real number, then
{\left[x\in\mathbb{Q}\right]}
denotes the
Dirichlet function
that sends every
x\in\mathbb{R}
to
1
if
x
is rational, and to
0
otherwise.

Lambda notation
\lambda x.E
, where
E
is some mathematical expression with free variable
x
, is used to introduce unnamed functions whose domain and codomain will be clear from the context.

3.2.
Fixed Point Theory

We introduce the foundations from fixed point theory
(
Winskel, 1993
, Section 5.4)
required to sensibly reason about the semantics of loops and to obtain suitable notions of quantitative loop invariants.

Let
(L,\,\preceq)
and
(M,\,\preceqq)
be partial orders.
A function
f\colon L\to M
is called
monotonic
, if for all
a\preceq b
, we have
f(a)\preceqq f(b)
.
An
\omega
-chain
in
(L,\,\preceq)
is a monotonic function
a\colon\mathbb{N}\to L
(where
\mathbb{N}
is ordered by the usual
\leq
relation), i.e., an
\omega
-chain is a non-decreasing sequence of elements
a(0)\preceq a(1)\preceq\ldots
from
L
.
The partial order
(L,\,\preceq)
is an
\omega
-complete partial order
(
\omega
-cpo, for short), if all
\omega
-chains
a
in
L
have a
supremum
(least upper bound)
\sup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}a(i)
in
(L,\,\preceq)
.
An
\omega
-cpo
(L,\,\preceq)
with an element
\bot\in L
satisfying
\bot\preceq a
for all
a
is called
\omega
-cpo with bottom
.
A function
f\colon L\to M
between
\omega
-cpos
(L,\,\preceq)
and
(M,\,\preceqq)
is called
\omega
-continuous
, if for all
\omega
-chains
a
in
L
it holds that
\sup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f(a(i))=f(\sup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}a(i))
.
Let
f\colon L\to L
be a function where
L
is an arbitrary set.
An element
a\in L
satisfying
f(a)=a
is called a
fixed point
of
f
.
The following is often attributed to Kleene:

Theorem 3.1 (Kleene’s Fixed Point Theorem
(
Winskel, 1993
, Theorem 5.11)
).

Let
(L,\,\preceq)
be an
\omega
-cpo with bottom and let
f\colon L\to L
be
\omega
-continuous.
Then
f
has a least fixed point
\operatorname{lfp}f\in L
and
\operatorname{lfp}f=\sup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f^{i}(\bot)
where
f^{i}(\bot)
denotes the
i
-fold application of
f
to
\bot
.

A partial order
(L,\,\preceq)
is said to be
\omega
-
co
complete
(
\omega
-cocpo) if all
\omega
-
co
chains
, i.e., non-
increasing
sequences
a(0)\succeq a(1)\succeq\ldots
in
L
have an infimum
\inf_{i\in\mathbb{N}}a(i)\in L
.
An
\omega
-cocpo with a greatest element
\top
is called
\omega
-cocpo
with top
.
Note that
(L,\,\preceq)
is an
\omega
-cocpo (with top) iff the reversed partial order
(L,\,\succeq)
is an
\omega
-cpo (with bottom).
Similarly, a function
f\colon L\to M
between
\omega
-cocpos
(L,\,\preceq)
and
(M,\,\preceqq)
is called
\omega
-
co
continuous
, if
\inf_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f(a(i))=f(\inf_{i\in\mathbb{N}}a(i))
for all non-increasing
a
.
Note that for an
\omega
-cocpo
(L,\,\preceq)
with top,
Theorem

3.1
reads as follows:
If
f\colon L\to L
is
\omega
-
co
continuous, then
f
has a
greatest
fixed point
\operatorname{gfp}f=\inf_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f^{i}(\top)
.

A partial order is called
\omega
-bicomplete
(
\omega
-bicpo), if it is both an
\omega
-cpo and
\omega
-cocpo.
Similarly, a function
f
between
\omega
-bicpos is called
\omega
-bicontinuous
, if it is both

\omega
-continuous and
\omega
-
co
continuous.

A partial order
(L,\,\preceq)
is a
complete lattice
if for all
A\subseteq L
there exists
\sup A\in L
and
\inf A\in L
.
Note that every complete lattice is an
\omega
-bicpo with bottom
\bot=\sup\emptyset
and top
\top=\inf\emptyset
.

Theorem 3.2 (Knaster-Tarski Theorem
(
Winskel, 1993
, Theorems 5.15 and 5.16)
).

Let
f\colon L\to L
be a monotonic function on the complete lattice
(L,\,\preceq)
.
Then
f
has a
least
and
greatest fixed point

\operatorname{lfp}f\in L
and
\operatorname{gfp}f\in L
.
Moreover, for all
a\in L
it holds that

\displaystyle f(a)\preceq a\quad\text{implies}\quad\operatorname{lfp}f\preceq a\quad\qquad\text{and}\qquad\quad a\preceq f(a)\quad\text{implies}\quad a\preceq\operatorname{gfp}f~.

The above implications are often referred to as
Park (co)induction

(
Park, 1969
)
.

4.
Weakest Pre-Expectations for Probabilistic Programs

In this section we define programming language
\mathsf{pWhile}
and its weakest pre-expectation semantics based on
Lebesgue
integrals as defined in
(
Szymczak and Katoen, 2019
)
.

4.1.
Program Syntax

For the rest of the paper we fix a finite
7
7

7

Once
V
is fixed we can only write programs with at most
|V|
distinct variables. However, as we never make any assumptions about the size of
V
, our theory applies to programs with arbitrarily many variables.
Previous work
(
Szymczak and Katoen, 2019
)
has considered an infinite
V
, but this requires defining a measure space on the infinite-dimensional
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{V}
, which is somewhat more involved.

set
V=\{\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y},\ldots\}
of program variables.
A
(program) state
is a variable valuation
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{V}
, where
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{V}
is a shorthand for the set of functions
V\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Notice that our program variables range over the
non-negative
reals.
The restriction to non-negative variables is for technical convenience and not essential, see
Remark

3
.

To obtain a well-defined weakest pre-expectation semantics of programs involving continuous sampling, we need to have some measure-theoretic fundamentals in mind, provided in
Section

A.2
.
Suffice it to say here that we consider the standard Borel
\sigma
-algebra and Lebesgue measure
\bm{\lambda}
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{V}
. Lebesgue integrals of a measurable function
f\colon\mathbb{R}\to{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
over a measurable set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}
are denoted by
\int_{A}f\,d\bm{\lambda}
, or
\int_{A}f(x)\,d\bm{\lambda}(x)
.
We explicitly allow Lebesgue integrals to evaluate to
\infty
.

Now let
\mathcal{E}
be a set of measurable functions of type
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{V}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and let
\mathcal{G}
be a set of measurable functions of type
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{V}\to\mathbb{B}
. Elements of
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{G}
are called
arithmetic expressions
and
guards
, resp.

Definition 4.1 (Probabilistic Programs).

Programs
C
in the set
\mathsf{pWhile}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{G})
of programs with arithmetic expressions from
\mathcal{E}
and guards from
\mathcal{G}
adhere to the following grammar:

\displaystyle C\quad\Coloneqq\quad

\displaystyle\mathtt{skip}

(effectless program)

\displaystyle~{}\mid{}~

\displaystyle\mathtt{diverge}

(nonterminating program)

\displaystyle~{}\mid{}~

\displaystyle\mathtt{x}\coloneq E

(assignment;
\mathtt{x}\in V,E\in\mathcal{E}
)

\displaystyle~{}\mid{}~

\displaystyle\mathtt{observe}(\varphi)

(conditioning;
\varphi\in\mathcal{G}
)

\displaystyle~{}\mid{}~

\displaystyle\mathtt{x}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}

(sample from real interval
{{[0,1]}}
;
\mathtt{x}\in V
)

\displaystyle~{}\mid{}~

\displaystyle\mathtt{if}(\varphi)~\{C\}~\mathtt{else}~\{C\}

(conditional choice;
\varphi\in\mathcal{G}
)

\displaystyle~{}\mid{}~

\displaystyle\{C\}~[p]~\{C\}

(probabilistic choice;
p\in{{[0,1]}}\cap\mathbb{Q}
)

\displaystyle~{}\mid{}~

\displaystyle C\,;\,C

(sequential composition)

\displaystyle~{}\mid{}~

\displaystyle\mathtt{while}~(\varphi)~\{C\}

(while loop;
\varphi\in\mathcal{G}
)

If
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{G}
are the sets of
all
measurable functions of the corresponding type, we write
\mathsf{pWhile}
instead of
\mathsf{pWhile}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{G})
.
A program not containing while-loops is called
loop-free
.

\triangle

Let us briefly go over each construct, all of which are standard.

\mathtt{skip}
does nothing.

\mathtt{diverge}
is a non-terminating program, i.e., behaves like
\mathtt{while}~(\mathsf{true})~\{\mathtt{skip}\}
.

\mathtt{x}\coloneq E
assigns the value of the arithmetic expression
E
evaluated in the current state to variable
\mathtt{x}
.

\mathtt{x}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}
assigns to
\mathtt{x}
a value drawn from the continuous uniform
{{[0,1]}}
-distribution.

\mathtt{observe}(\varphi)

conditions
the program execution on the guard
\varphi
being true.

\{C_{1}\}~[p]~\{C_{2}\}
executes
C_{1}
with probability
p
, otherwise
C_{2}
.
The assumption
p\in{{[0,1]}}\cap\mathbb{Q}
is to avoid defining a dedicated syntax for probabilities later on in
Section

8
.

\mathtt{if}(\varphi)~\{C_{1}\}~\mathtt{else}~\{C_{2}\}
,
C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}
, and
\mathtt{while}~(\varphi)~\{C\}
are standard conditional choices, sequential compositions, and while-loops, respectively.
See
Section

B.1
for additional remarks.

Remark 1 (More General Distributions).

In theory, the restriction to uniform
{[0,1]}
-distributions does not limit expressiveness:
Arbitrary distributions can be simulated by sampling from
{[0,1]}
and applying the inverse

cumulative distribution function
(CDF) of the target distribution
(
Szymczak and Katoen, 2019
)
.
However, to obtain decidability results, we further restrict the syntax of arithmetic expressions and guards to a class of functions expressible in first-order (FO) real arithmetic, see
Section

8
.
Distributions whose inverse CDF belongs to this class include triangular, trapezoidal, U-quadratic, and Kumaraswamy distributions.
On the other hand, distributions with transcendental inverse CDF (Gaussian, Laplace, etc.) do not reside in this class.
We mention two future directions to address this issue:
(i) leverage heuristics implemented in modern SMT solvers to discharge the generated verification conditions, even if they do not belong to a decidable theory,
(ii) soundly over/under-approximate the transcendental inverse CDF by FO-expressible algebraic functions.

Remark 2 (Soft Conditioning).

Our syntax has native support for
hard conditioning
(
\mathtt{observe}
). Bounded
soft conditioning
(scoring) — multiplying the current execution with a weight in
{{[0,1]}}
— can be simulated using
\mathtt{x}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}
and
\mathtt{observe}
, see
(
Szymczak and Katoen, 2019
, Lemma 5)
for details.

4.2.
Weakest Pre-Expectation Semantics

Table 1
.

)
.

\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\left(\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\left(f\right)\right)

\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\left(\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket\left(f\right)\right)
