\operatorname{lfp}\lambda Y.\,{\left[\neg\varphi\right]}\cdot f+{\left[\varphi\right]}\cdot\mathsf{wp}\llbracket B\rrbracket\left(Y\right)

\operatorname{gfp}\lambda Y.\,{\left[\neg\varphi\right]}\cdot f+{\left[\varphi\right]}\cdot\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket B\rrbracket\left(Y\right)

We now unify the weakest pre-expectation calculi for continuous probabilistic programs from
(
Szymczak and Katoen, 2019
)
with the calculi proposed in
(
Olmedo et al., 2018
)
. The latter calculi take
renormalization
— for conditional expected outcomes — into account.
The central objects these calculi operate on are
expectations
:

Definition 4.2 (Expectations).

We distinguish between the following sets of functions:

(1)

The set of
expectations
is
\mathbb{E}=\{f\mid f\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{V}\to{\overline{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}\}
.

(2)

The set of
1-bounded expectations
is
{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}}=\{f\mid f\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{V}\to{{[0,1]}}\}
.

(3)

The set of
measurable (1-bounded) expectations

{\mathbb{E}_{meas}}
(
{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}_{meas}}
) is the subset of
\mathbb{E}
(
{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}}
) containing exactly the Borel-measurable functions.

We equip all of these sets with the partial order
\sqsubseteq
defined as
f\sqsubseteq g
iff
\forall\sigma\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{V}\colon f(\sigma)\leq g(\sigma)
.

\triangle

Crucially, we have (see
(
Szymczak and Katoen, 2019
, Lemma 2)
and
Section

C.3
):

Lemma 4.3.

(\mathbb{E},\,\sqsubseteq)
and
({\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}},\,\sqsubseteq)
are complete lattices.

({\mathbb{E}_{meas}},\,\sqsubseteq)
and
({\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}_{meas}},\,\sqsubseteq)
are
\omega
-bicpos with bottom (
0{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{}=\lambda\sigma.0}
) and top (
\infty{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{}=\lambda\sigma.\infty}
and
1{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,0.4}{}=\lambda\sigma.1}
, respectively).

The arithmetic operations
+
(addition) and
\cdot
(multiplication) on
\mathbb{E}
are defined pointwise, i.e.,
\forall\sigma\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{V}\colon(f+g)(\sigma)=f(\sigma)+g(\sigma)
, and analogously for multiplication.
These operations preserve measurability
(
Rudin, 1953
, Theorem 11.18)
, i.e.,
{\mathbb{E}_{meas}}
is closed under
+
and
\cdot
.
Moreover, addition (for both arguments) and multiplication by constants
8
8

8

Formally, for every
g\in\mathbb{E}
these are the functions
\lambda f.f+g
and
\lambda f.f\cdot g
.

are
\omega
-bicontinuous functions.

For state
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{V}
, program variable
\mathtt{x}\in V
,
\xi\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we define the
updated state as

\sigma[\mathtt{x}\mapsto\xi]~{}={}~\lambda\mathtt{y}.\begin{cases}\xi&\text{if $\mathtt{y}=\mathtt{x}$}\\
\sigma(\mathtt{y})&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}~.

Further, given an expectation
f\in\mathbb{E}
, a variable
\mathtt{x}\in V
, and an arithmetic expression
E\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{V}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
we define the
substitution of
\mathtt{x}
by
E
in
f
as the expectation
f[\mathtt{x}/E]=\lambda\sigma.f(\sigma[\mathtt{x}\mapsto E(\sigma)])
.
If we have syntactic expressions for
f
and
E
, then we can obtain a syntactic representation of
f[\mathtt{x}/E]
by substituting all free occurrences of
\mathtt{x}
in
f
by
E
in a capture-avoiding manner, see
Section

8
.

Definition 4.4 (Weakest (Liberal) Pre-expectation Transformers
(
Szymczak and Katoen, 2019
)
).

For all
C\in\mathsf{pWhile}
, the
weakest (liberal) pre-expectation transformers

\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\colon{\mathbb{E}_{meas}}\to{\mathbb{E}_{meas}}
and
\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\colon{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}_{meas}}\to{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}_{meas}}
are defined inductively on the structure of
C
according to the rules in
Table

1
.

\triangle

Let us briefly explain the inductive definition in
Table

1
.
The effectless program
\mathtt{skip}
leaves the post-expectation unchanged.
An assignment
\mathtt{x}\coloneq E
substitutes the expression
E
for the variable
\mathtt{x}
in the post-expectation
f
, while uniform assignment
\mathtt{x}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}
integrates the expectation over all possible values of
\mathtt{x}
in the interval
{{[0,1]}}
, capturing the averaging effect of drawing
\mathtt{x}
uniformly.

\mathtt{observe}(\varphi)
scales the expectation by the Iverson bracket of the guard
\varphi
.
Proper renormalization is performed in a second step, see
Section

4.4
.
For
\mathtt{if}(\varphi)~\{C_{1}\}~\mathtt{else}~\{C_{2}\}
and the probabilistic choice
\{C_{1}\}~[p]~\{C_{2}\}
, the weakest (liberal) pre-expectation is a weighted sum of the
\mathsf{wp}
’s from both branches, either weighted by
{\left[\varphi\right]}
and
{\left[\neg\varphi\right]}
in the former case, or by the probabilities
p
and
1-p
in the latter.
The
\mathsf{w}(\mathsf{l})\mathsf{p}
of a sequential composition
C_{1}\,;\,C_{2}
is function composition
\mathsf{w}(\mathsf{l})\mathsf{p}\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket\circ\mathsf{w}(\mathsf{l})\mathsf{p}\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket
.
Notably,
C_{2}
is evaluated
before

C_{1}
—
\mathsf{w}(\mathsf{l})\mathsf{p}
’s are thus computed in a backward manner.
Note that
\mathsf{wp}
and
\mathsf{wlp}
only differ in the handling of divergence and loops.
The
\mathtt{diverge}
command, representing non-termination, sets the
\mathsf{wp}
to
0
and the
\mathsf{wlp}
to
1
.
The
\mathtt{while}~(\varphi)~\{C\}
loop involves computing the
least
fixed point (
\operatorname{lfp}
) for
\mathsf{wp}
and the
greatest
fixed point (
\operatorname{gfp}
) for
\mathsf{wlp}
.
The difference between
\mathsf{wp}
and
\mathsf{wlp}
is further explained in
Section

4.3
.

Remark 3 (On Non-Negativity).

We follow the classic line of work on weakest pre-expectations
(
Kozen, 1983
;
McIver and Morgan, 2005
)
and restrict attention to
non-negative expectations
(see
(
Kaminski and Katoen, 2017
)
for a discussion of the mixed-sign case).
This means that we can only reason about expected values of non-negative random variables measured in a program’s final state.
As a consequence, in order to reason about the expected final value of a program variable
\mathtt{x}
on termination, we have to assume that
\mathtt{x}
is unsigned.
We have opted to ensure this by simply requiring
all
variables to be unsigned real numbers.

Given a loop
C=\mathtt{while}~(\varphi)~\{B\}
and
f\in{\mathbb{E}_{meas}}
, we denote by

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-11.69948pt{\mathsf{wp}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-11.69948pt{\mathsf{wp}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-7.85pt{\mathsf{wp}}\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-6.40187pt{C}\kern 3.5389pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-6.83888pt{\mathsf{wp}}\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-5.39075pt{C}\kern 2.52777pt{{f}}}}}\colon{\mathbb{E}_{meas}}\to{\mathbb{E}_{meas}}\,,\qquad\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-11.69948pt{\mathsf{wp}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-11.69948pt{\mathsf{wp}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-7.85pt{\mathsf{wp}}\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-6.40187pt{C}\kern 3.5389pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-6.83888pt{\mathsf{wp}}\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-5.39075pt{C}\kern 2.52777pt{{f}}}}}(g)~{}={}~{\left[\varphi\right]}\cdot\mathsf{wp}\llbracket B\rrbracket\left(g\right)+{\left[\neg\varphi\right]}\cdot f

the
\mathsf{wp}
-characteristic function of
C
w.r.t.
f
(analogously for
f^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}_{meas}}
and
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wlp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-12.87003pt{\mathsf{wlp}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{f^{\prime}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wlp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-12.87003pt{\mathsf{wlp}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{f^{\prime}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wlp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-8.68611pt{\mathsf{wlp}}\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-6.40187pt{C}\kern 3.5389pt{{f^{\prime}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wlp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-7.67499pt{\mathsf{wlp}}\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-5.39075pt{C}\kern 2.52777pt{{f^{\prime}}}}}}
).
Hence, weakest pre-expectations of loops can be denoted more concisely as

\displaystyle\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)~{}={}~\operatorname{lfp}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-11.69948pt{\mathsf{wp}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-11.69948pt{\mathsf{wp}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-7.85pt{\mathsf{wp}}\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-6.40187pt{C}\kern 3.5389pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-6.83888pt{\mathsf{wp}}\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-5.39075pt{C}\kern 2.52777pt{{f}}}}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f^{\prime}\right)~{}={}~\operatorname{gfp}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wlp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-12.87003pt{\mathsf{wlp}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{f^{\prime}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wlp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-12.87003pt{\mathsf{wlp}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{f^{\prime}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wlp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-8.68611pt{\mathsf{wlp}}\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-6.40187pt{C}\kern 3.5389pt{{f^{\prime}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathsf{wlp}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-7.67499pt{\mathsf{wlp}}\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-5.39075pt{C}\kern 2.52777pt{{f^{\prime}}}}}}~.

Due to the fixed points in
Table

1
it is not immediately obvious that
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket
and
\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket
are well-defined.
This will we be ensured by Kleene’s
Theorem

3.1
as shown in the next lemma (parts of which have been proved in
(
Szymczak and Katoen, 2019
)
).

Theorem 4.5 (Well-definedness of
\mathsf{wp}
and
\mathsf{wlp}
).

For all programs
C\in\mathsf{pWhile}
,
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket
and
\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket
are well-defined.
In particular,
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket
is
\omega
-continuous and
\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket
is
\omega
-
co
continuous.

4.3.
Probabilistic Termination:
\mathsf{wp}
vs.
\mathsf{wlp}

In general, for all
C\in\mathsf{pWhile}
and
f\in{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}_{meas}}
we have
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)\sqsubseteq\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)
since the former relies on a least and the latter on a greatest fixed point.
More specifically, we have
(
Szymczak and Katoen, 2019
, Section 5)

(1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)+\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(0\right)~{}={}~\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)~.

Let us make the meaning of
\mathsf{wp}
and
\mathsf{wlp}
w.r.t. the constant post-expectations
0
and
1
explicit.
Assuming
C
is started with initial state
\sigma
we have the following:

9
9

9

These statements can be formalized by means of an operational semantics for
\mathsf{pWhile}
, see
(
Szymczak and Katoen, 2019
)
.

•

\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(0\right)(\sigma)
is always 0.

•

\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(1\right)(\sigma)
is the probability of
termination
without violating any
\mathtt{observe}
.

•

\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(0\right)(\sigma)
is the probability of
non-termination
without violating any
\mathtt{observe}
.

•

\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(1\right)(\sigma)
is the probability to never violate any
\mathtt{observe}
.

We call
C

almost-surely terminating
(AST) if
\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(0\right)=0
, i.e., if the program does not admit any initial state for which the program’s infinite runs that do not violate any
\mathtt{observe}
have positive probability mass.
It follows from equation (
1
) that
C
is AST iff
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)=\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)
.

4.4.
Conditional Weakest Pre-Expectations

For a program
C\in\mathsf{pWhile}
, an expectation
f\in{\mathbb{E}_{meas}}
and a state
\sigma
, following
(
Olmedo et al., 2018
)
, we define:

\mathsf{cwp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)(\sigma)~{}={}~\begin{cases}\frac{\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)(\sigma)}{\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(1\right)(\sigma)}&\text{ if }\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(1\right)(\sigma)\neq 0\\[5.0pt]
\text{undefined}&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

The above definition factors out the probability mass of runs violating an
\mathtt{observe}
, i.e., divides by
\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(1\right)(\sigma)
, see
Section

4.3
.

\mathsf{cwp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)(\sigma)
is thus the expected value of
f
after termination of
C
started with initial state
\sigma
,
conditioned
on all
\mathtt{observe}
statements in
C
being successful.

5.
Approximate Riemann Weakest Pre-Expectations

We start by recalling lower and upper Riemann sums and integrals.
10
10

10

The definition of an integral in terms of these sums is in fact commonly attributed to Darboux, not to Riemann.
However, Darboux’s integral is equivalent to Riemann’s which, rather than considering lower and upper sums, relies on evaluating
f
at sample points within the intervals of a partition of the integration domain.
We refer to
(
Burk, 2007
, Chapter 3, Theorems 3.3.1 and 3.3.2)
for an in-depth comparison.
In this paper, we consistently use Darboux’s definitions, but refer to them nonetheless as Riemann sums and integrals, since the latter terminology is more widespread.

Let
{[a,b]}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
and
N\geq 1
.
A
partition
of
{[a,b]}
is a tuple of at least
N+1
real numbers
P=(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
such that

a=x_{0}~{}<{}~x_{1}~{}<{}~\ldots~{}<{}~x_{N}=b~.

The set of all partitions of the interval
{[a,b]}
is denoted
\mathfrak{P}{[a,b]}
.
For a bounded
f\colon{[a,b]}\to\mathbb{R}
and a partition
P=(x_{0},\ldots,x_{N})\in\mathfrak{P}{[a,b]}
, we define the
lower
and
upper sums
of
f
w.r.t.
P
as

{L_{f,P}}~{}={}~\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_{i}-x_{i-1})\inf_{\xi\in{[x_{i-1},x_{i}]}}f(\xi)\qquad\text{and}\qquad{U_{f,P}}~{}={}~\sum_{i=1}^{N}(x_{i}-x_{i-1})\sup_{\xi\in{[x_{i-1},x_{i}]}}f(\xi)~.

Note that
{U_{f,P}}
and
{L_{f,P}}
are well-defined real numbers because
f
is bounded.

Definition 5.1 (Riemann integral).

Let
f\colon{[a,b]}\to\mathbb{R}
be bounded.
The
lower-
and
upper Riemann integrals
of
f
are defined as follows:

\underline{\int_{a}^{b}}f(x)\,dx~{}={}~\sup\left\{{L_{f,P}}\mid P\in\mathfrak{P}{[a,b]}\right\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overline{\int_{a}^{b}}f(x)\,dx~{}={}~\inf\left\{{U_{f,P}}\mid P\in\mathfrak{P}{[a,b]}\right\}~.

If the upper integral equals the lower integral, then the common value is written
\int_{a}^{b}f(x)\,dx
and called
the
Riemann integral of
f
.
In this case,
f
is called
Riemann-integrable
.

\triangle

Finally, we introduce the following terminology:
(i) For
g\colon D\to\mathbb{R}
,
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, we say that
g
is Riemann-integrable
on an interval

{[c,d]}\subset D
if the restriction
g\colon{[c,d]}\to\mathbb{R}
is Riemann-integrable.
(ii) In this paper we often consider functions of the form
g\colon D^{V}\to\mathbb{R}
where
V
is a finite set, e.g. the set of program variables.
We then say that
g
is Riemann-integrable on
{[c,d]}\subseteq D
w.r.t. some
\mathtt{x}\in V
if
for all

\sigma\in D^{V}
the function
\lambda\xi.g(\sigma[\mathtt{x}\mapsto\xi])
of type
{[c,d]}\to\mathbb{R}
is Riemann-integrable.

Example 5.2.

To illustrate the concepts defined above consider the following:

•

A constant function
f\colon{[a,b]}\to\mathbb{R},x\mapsto c
for
c\in\mathbb{R}
is Riemann-integrable because for
all
partitions
P
we have
{L_{f,P}}={U_{f,P}}=c(a-b)
.

•

f(x,y)={\left[x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\right]}
is Riemann-integrable on
{[0,1]}
w.r.t.
x
.
Indeed, for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
\int_{0}^{1}f(x,y)\,dx={\left[-1\leq y\leq 1\right]}\sqrt{1-y^{2}}
.

•

The Dirichlet function
f\colon{[a,b]}\to\mathbb{R},x\mapsto{\left[x\in\mathbb{Q}\right]}
is not Riemann-integrable because for all partitions
P
we have
{L_{f,P}}=0
and
{U_{f,P}}=1
.
Note that
f
is nowhere continuous.

5.1.
\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N}
and
\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}
: Lower and Upper Riemann Weakest Pre-Expectations

We are now ready to define our approximate expectation transformers.
They arise from the standard transformers defined in
Table

1
by replacing the
Lebesgue
integrals in the
\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}
case by a lower or upper Riemann sum.
Formally:

Definition 5.3 (Lower and Upper Riemann
\mathsf{w}(\mathsf{l})\mathsf{p}
-Transformers).

For all
C\in\mathsf{pWhile}
and integers
N\geq 1
, the expectation transformers
\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\colon\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{E}
and
\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\colon\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{E}
are defined by induction over the structure of
C
as in
Table

1
, with the only exception that if
C
is
\mathtt{x}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}
, then for all
f\in\mathbb{E}
we set
11
11

11

Notice that
\inf_{\xi\in{[a,b]}}f[\mathtt{x}/\xi]
is the same as
\lambda\sigma.\inf_{\xi\in{[a,b]}}f(\sigma[\mathtt{x}\mapsto\xi])
.

\displaystyle\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket\mathtt{x}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}\rrbracket\left(f\right)~{}={}~\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\inf_{\xi\in[\frac{i}{N},\frac{i+1}{N}]}f[\mathtt{x}/\xi]\qquad\text{and, similarly,}

\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}\llbracket\mathtt{x}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}\rrbracket\left(f\right)~{}={}~\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\sup_{\xi\in[\frac{i}{N},\frac{i+1}{N}]}f[\mathtt{x}/\xi]~.

The lower and upper Riemann weakest
liberal
pre-expectation transformers
\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\colon{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}}\to{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}}
and
\overline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{l}\overline{\mathsf{p}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\colon{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}}\to{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}}
are defined analogously.

\triangle

Our Riemann transformers approximate the Lebesgue integral in the definition of
\mathsf{wp}
(and
\mathsf{wlp}
) by a lower or upper Riemann sum.
We work with the partitions
0<\tfrac{1}{N}<\tfrac{2}{N}<\ldots<1
of the unit interval for the sake of concreteness.
Note that these partitions are
not
successive refinements of each other (see
Lemma

A.3
for definitions).
Thus, increasing
N
by
1
does
not necessarily
yield
“better”
approximations, i.e.,
we might have

\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)\not\sqsubseteq\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N+1}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)
, e.g:

\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{2}\llbracket\mathtt{x}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}\rrbracket\left({\left[x\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\right]}\right)~\not\sqsubseteq~\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{3}\llbracket\mathtt{x}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}\rrbracket\left({\left[x\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\right]}\right)~.

Given a loop
C=\mathtt{while}~(\varphi)~\{B\}
,
N\geq 1
,
f\in\mathbb{E}
, and
\mathcal{T}\in\{\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N},\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\}
, we define the
\mathcal{T}
-characteristic function of
C
w.r.t.
f
as

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-9.35837pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-9.35837pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-6.17778pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-6.40187pt{C}\kern 3.5389pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-5.16666pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-5.39075pt{C}\kern 2.52777pt{{f}}}}}\colon\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{E},\qquad\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-9.35837pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-9.35837pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-6.17778pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-6.40187pt{C}\kern 3.5389pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-5.16666pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-5.39075pt{C}\kern 2.52777pt{{f}}}}}(g)~{}={}~{\left[\varphi\right]}\cdot\mathcal{T}\llbracket B\rrbracket\left(g\right)+{\left[\neg\varphi\right]}\cdot f~.

For
g\in{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}}
and
\mathcal{T}\in\{\overline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{l}\overline{\mathsf{p}}^{N},\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\}
,
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-9.35837pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{g}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-9.35837pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{g}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-6.17778pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-6.40187pt{C}\kern 3.5389pt{{g}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-5.16666pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-5.39075pt{C}\kern 2.52777pt{{g}}}}}\colon{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}}\to{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}}

we analogously define

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-9.35837pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{g}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-9.35837pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{g}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-6.17778pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-6.40187pt{C}\kern 3.5389pt{{g}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-5.16666pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-5.39075pt{C}\kern 2.52777pt{{g}}}}}\colon{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}}\to{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}},\qquad\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-9.35837pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{g}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-9.35837pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{g}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-6.17778pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-6.40187pt{C}\kern 3.5389pt{{g}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\mathcal{T}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-5.16666pt{\mathcal{T}}\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-5.39075pt{C}\kern 2.52777pt{{g}}}}}(g)~{}={}~{\left[\varphi\right]}\cdot\mathcal{T}\llbracket B\rrbracket\left(g\right)+{\left[\neg\varphi\right]}\cdot g~.

Note that unlike
\mathsf{wp}
and
\mathsf{wlp}
, the approximate transformers are defined on the full sets of expectations
\mathbb{E}
and
{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}}
, respectively, not just on the measurable ones.
All transformers from
Definition

5.3
are well-defined: this follows from the Knaster-Tarski
Theorem

3.2
using that
\mathbb{E}
and
{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}}
are complete lattices and monotonicity of the transformers, see
Lemma

5.4
below.

5.2.
Healthiness Properties of
\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N}
and
\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}

Lemma 5.4 (Monotonicity of the Riemann
\mathsf{w}(\mathsf{l})\mathsf{p}
-Transformers).

For all
C\in\mathsf{pWhile}
and integers
N\geq 1
, the functions
\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket
,
\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket
,
\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket
, and
\overline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{l}\overline{\mathsf{p}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket
are monotonic w.r.t.
\sqsubseteq
.

Notably, the Riemann
\mathsf{w}(\mathsf{l})\mathsf{p}
-transformers do not possess the same continuity properties as their Lebesgue counterparts from
Section

4.2
.
For instance, due to the presence of infima in the lower Riemann sum,
\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket
is
not

\omega
-continuous in general (see
Section

C.6
for a counter-example).

Lemma 5.5 (Soundness of the Riemann
\mathsf{w}(\mathsf{l})\mathsf{p}
-Transformers).

For all programs
C\in\mathsf{pWhile}
, post-expectations
f\in{\mathbb{E}_{meas}}
,
g\in{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}_{meas}}
, and integers
N\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)

\displaystyle~{}\sqsubseteq{}~\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)~{}\sqsubseteq{}~\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)\quad\text{and}\quad

\displaystyle\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(g\right)

\displaystyle~{}\sqsubseteq{}~\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(g\right)~{}\sqsubseteq{}~\overline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{l}\overline{\mathsf{p}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(g\right)~.

For
conditional
weakest pre-expectations (
Section

4.4
), we immediately get the following:

Corollary 5.6.

For all programs
C\in\mathsf{pWhile}
, post-expectations
f\in{\mathbb{E}_{meas}}
, and integers
N\geq 1

\displaystyle\frac{\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)(\sigma)}{\overline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{l}\overline{\mathsf{p}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(1\right)(\sigma)}~{}\leq{}~\mathsf{cwp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)(\sigma)~{}\leq{}~\frac{\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)(\sigma)}{\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(1\right)(\sigma)}

for all
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{V}
such that none of
\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(1\right)(\sigma)
,
\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(1\right)(\sigma)
, and
\overline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{l}\overline{\mathsf{p}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(1\right)(\sigma)
is zero.

We stress that
Lemma

5.5
and
Corollary

5.6
hold even for non-Riemann-integrable post-expectations.

6.
Invariant-Based Reasoning for Loops

Deductive probabilistic program verification techniques typically bound expected outcomes of loops by means of
quantitative loop invariants
. Intuitively, a quantitative loop invariant
I
is an expectation whose pre-expectation w.r.t.
one loop iteration
does not increase (or decrease, depending on whether one wants to establish upper or lower bones, see below).
While quantitative loop invariant-based reasoning can simplify the verification of loops significantly,
the continuous setting poses challenges: Computing the pre-expectation of
I
w.r.t. a loop’s body requires reasoning about possibly complex Lebesgue integrals — involving, e.g., indicator functions of predicates — arising from the continuous sampling instructions.
We now develop invariant-based proof rules for our
Riemann pre-expectations
(
Definition

5.3
), which yield sound bounds on the
Lebesgue pre-expectations
(
Definition

4.4
).
We will see in
Sections

8
and
9
that these proof rules give rise to SMT-based techniques for verifying bounds on Lebesgue pre-expectations of loops in a semi-automated fashion.

Our first insight is that — due to
(\mathbb{E},\,\sqsubseteq)
and
({\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}},\,\sqsubseteq)
being complete lattices and the Riemann expectation transformers being monotonic by
Lemma

5.4
— bounds on
Riemann
pre-expectations of loops can be established via Park induction (
Theorem

3.2
):

Lemma 6.1.

Let
C=\mathtt{while}~(\varphi)~\{B\}\in\mathsf{pWhile}
and
N\geq 1
. Then:

(1)

For all
f,I\in\mathbb{E}
, we have

\underbrace{\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-14.51323pt{\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-14.51323pt{\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-12.34377pt{\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}}\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-6.40187pt{C}\kern 3.5389pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-11.33264pt{\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}}\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-5.39075pt{C}\kern 2.52777pt{{f}}}}}(I)\sqsubseteq I}_{\text{$I$ is $\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}$-superinvariant of $C$ w.r.t.\ $f$}}\qquad\text{implies}\qquad\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)\sqsubseteq I~.

(2)

For all
f,I\in{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}}
, we have

\underbrace{I\sqsubseteq\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-17.04489pt{\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-17.04489pt{\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}}\kern 5.70836pt}}_{{\kern-9.67207pt{C}\kern 5.70836pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-14.1521pt{\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}}\kern 3.5389pt}}_{{\kern-6.40187pt{C}\kern 3.5389pt{{f}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}}}}_{{{C}}}}\Phi^{{\kern-13.14098pt{\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}}\kern 2.52777pt}}_{{\kern-5.39075pt{C}\kern 2.52777pt{{f}}}}}(I)}_{\text{$I$ is $\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}$-subinvariant of $C$ w.r.t.\ $f$}}\qquad\text{implies}\qquad I\sqsubseteq\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)~.

More colloquially stated,
super
invariants yield
upper
bounds on
upper
Riemann pre-expectations of loops and, dually,
sub
invariants yield
lower
bounds on
lower liberal
Riemann pre-expectations. Notice that establishing the premise of the above proof rules only requires reasoning about the loop’s body and avoids explicitly computing Lebesgue integrals.

It thus follows from the soundness of our Riemann expectation transformers (
Lemma

5.5
) that the above proof rules yield sound bounds on
Lebesgue
pre-expectations.

Theorem 6.2.

Let
C=\mathtt{while}~(\varphi)~\{B\}\in\mathsf{pWhile}
and
N,N^{\prime}\geq 1
. We have:

(1)

If
I\in\mathbb{E}
is a
\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}
-superinvariant of
C
w.r.t.
f\in{\mathbb{E}_{meas}}
, then

\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)\sqsubseteq I~
.

(2)

If
I\in{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}}
is a
\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}
-subinvariant of
C
w.r.t.
f\in{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}_{meas}}
, then

I\sqsubseteq\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)
.

(3)

If
I\in\mathbb{E}
is a
\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}
-superinvariant of
C
w.r.t.
f\in{\mathbb{E}_{meas}}
and
J\in{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}}
is a
\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N^{\prime}}
-subinvariant of
C
w.r.t.
1
, then, for all
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{V}
,
J(\sigma)>0
implies that
\mathsf{cwp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)(\sigma)
is defined and

\mathsf{cwp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)(\sigma)~{}\leq{}~\frac{I(\sigma)}{J(\sigma)}~.

It is important to note that in
Lemma

6.1
we admit
arbitrary
(
1
-bounded) post-expectations whereas in
Theorem

6.2
we have to restrict to
measurable
(
1
-bounded) post-expectations in order for the Lebesgue pre-expectations
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)
and
\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)
to be well-defined.
The quantitative loop invariant
I
, on the other hand, must
not
necessarily be measurable since there is no need to plug
I
into a Lebesgue expectation transformer.

Example 6.3.

Consider the Monte Carlo
\pi
-approximator
C
from
Figure

1
.
The expectation

I~{}={}~\mathtt{count}+{\left[\mathtt{i}\leq\mathtt{M}\right]}\cdot\big(0.85\cdot((\mathtt{M}\mathbin{\dot{-}}\mathtt{i})+1)\big)

is a
\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{16}
-superinvariant of
C
w.r.t.
\mathtt{count}
(here
\mathbin{\dot{-}}
denote the
monus
operator defined as
\mathsf{t}_{1}\mathbin{\dot{-}}\mathsf{t}_{2}=\max(\mathsf{t}_{1}-\mathsf{t}_{2},0)
, detailed in
Section

8.1
).
Hence, we get by
Theorem

6.2
,

\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{count}\right)\sqsubseteq I
,
i.e., if initially
\mathtt{count}=0,\mathtt{i}=1
, then
0.85\cdot\mathtt{M}
upper-bounds the expected final value of
\mathtt{count}
.

7.
Convergence of The Riemann Weakest Pre-Expectations

We now address the question if and under which conditions
\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)
and
\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)
converge to
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)
as
N
goes to
\infty
.
We focus on
pointwise
convergence.
It is easy to see that in general, pointwise convergence does not hold: consider for example the simple program
C=\mathtt{x}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}
and the expectation
f={\left[\mathtt{x}\in\mathbb{Q}\right]}
, for which we have
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)=0
.
However, for every
N\geq 1
it holds that
\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)=1
.
Our goal is thus to identify a
Riemann-suitable
subset of our framework, by mildly restricting the class of allowed expectations.

We first recall two fundamental properties of the Riemann integral.
The first is a well-known characterization of Riemann integrability, the second reveals that Lebesgue integrals conservatively generalize Riemann integrals.
In the following we will use
\bm{\lambda}
to denote the Lebesgue measure.
For the formal definition and properties of
\bm{\lambda}
we refer the reader to
Appendix

A
.

Theorem 7.1 (Riemann-Lebesgue Theorem

(
Rudin, 1953
, Thm. 11.33)
).

Let
f\colon{[a,b]}\to\mathbb{R}
be bounded.
Consider the set
D=\{x\in{[a,b]}\mid f\text{ is discontinuous in }x\}
.
Then
f
is Riemann-integrable if and only if
\bm{\lambda}(D)=0
, i.e.,
f
is
continuous almost everywhere
.

Theorem 7.2 (

(
Ash and Doléans-Dade, 2000
, Theorem 1.7.1)
).

Let
f\colon{[a,b]}\to\mathbb{R}
be bounded and Riemann-integrable.
Then

\int_{a}^{b}f(x)\,dx~{}={}~\int_{{[a,b]}}f(x)\,d\bm{\lambda}(x)~,

i.e., the Riemann integral coincides with the Lebesgue integral.

7.1.
Convergence of Approximation for Loop-free Programs

By
Theorems

7.1
and
7.2
, the Riemann integral coincides with the Lebesgue integral for
“sufficiently continuous”
functions.
There is thus hope to establish convergence if we restrict our setting to almost everywhere continuous expectations.
However, there is an additional complication: A function needs to be
bounded
on the integration domain in order to be Riemann-integrable – otherwise the upper sum could be
“stuck”
at
\infty
.
We address this issue using the concept of
local boundedness
:
A function
f\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{V}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
is called
locally bounded
if

\text{for all compact }K\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{V}\colon\quad\sup_{\sigma\in K}|f(\sigma)|~<~\infty~.

We recall that a subset
K
of
\mathbb{R}^{V}
is compact iff it is closed and bounded.
Many common functions such as polynomials are locally bounded.
An example of a function that is
not
locally bounded is
{\left[x>0\right]}\cdot\tfrac{1}{x}
.
Based on these considerations, we define the following:

Definition 7.3 (Riemann-suitable).

A combination
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})
of a class
\mathcal{E}
of locally bounded arithmetic expressions, a class
\mathcal{G}
of guards, and a class
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathbb{E}
of locally bounded expectations if
for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
and
\mathtt{x}\in V
,
f
is Riemann-integrable on
{{[0,1]}}
w.r.t.
\mathtt{x}
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{F}
contains the constant expectations
0,1
and satisfies the following closure properties:

(i)

\mathcal{F}
is closed under taking infima and suprema over closed subintervals of
{{[0,1]}}
, i.e., for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
,
\mathtt{x}\in V
, and
{[a,b]}\subseteq{{[0,1]}}
we have

\inf_{\xi\in{[a,b]}}f[\mathtt{x}/\xi]\in\mathcal{F}
and
\sup_{\xi\in{[a,b]}}f[\mathtt{x}/\xi]\in\mathcal{F}
.

(ii)

For all
f\in\mathcal{F}
,
E\in\mathcal{E}
and
\mathtt{x}\in V
, we have
f[\mathtt{x}/E]\in\mathcal{F}
.

(iii)

For all
f,g\in\mathcal{F}
and
p\in{{[0,1]}}\cap\mathbb{Q}
, we have
p\cdot f+(1-p)\cdot g\in\mathcal{F}
.

(iv)

For all
f,g\in\mathcal{F}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{G}
, we have
{\left[\varphi\right]}\cdot f+{\left[\neg\varphi\right]}\cdot g\in\mathcal{F}
.

\triangle

The conditions (i) - (iv) in the above definition ensure that
\mathcal{F}
is closed under
\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket
and
\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket
for
loop-free

C
(see (
2
) in
Lemma

7.4
below).
We remark that it is not immediately clear if any
“interesting”
Riemann-suitable instantiations exist.
Fortunately, the answer is
yes
, as we show in
Section

8
.
In the Riemann-suitable setting, we can show the following convergence result:

Lemma 7.4 (Convergence – Loop-free Case).

Let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})
be Riemann-suitable.
Then for all loop-free
C\in\mathsf{pWhile}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{G})
and post-expectations
f\in\mathcal{F}
the following holds:

(2)

\displaystyle\forall N\geq 1\colon\quad\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)\in\mathcal{F}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)\in\mathcal{F}~.

(3)

\displaystyle\text{If }f\in{\mathbb{E}_{meas}}\colon\quad\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)~{}={}~\sup_{N\geq 1}\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)~{}={}~\inf_{N\geq 1}\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)~.

An analogous
\mathsf{wlp}
-version of
Lemma

7.4
is stated in
Section

C.11
.

7.2.
Convergence of Approximation for Programs with Loops

Recall the
Dirichlet program

D
from
Section

2
.
For all
N
we have
\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}\llbracket D\rrbracket\left(1\right)=1
, showing that
Lemma

7.4
does not hold for loopy programs in general.
The added difficulty stems from the fact that the semantics of loops is itself a limit.
Specifically, we can imagine to
“unroll”
every loop in a program
C
up to a certain depth
0pt\in\mathbb{N}
, denoted
C^{0pt}
(see
Section

B.2
for the formal definition), and view the semantics
C
as the limit of the semantics of
C^{0pt}
when
0pt
tends to infinity.
Notably,
\mathsf{wp}
and
\mathsf{wlp}
behave differently with respect to this limit: when increasing
0pt
the (non-liberal) pre-expectation of
f
increases, while the liberal pre-expectation decreases.
This is formalized in the next lemma:

Lemma 7.5.

For all
C\in\mathsf{pWhile}
and post-expectations
f\in{\mathbb{E}_{meas}}
and
g\in{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}_{meas}}
,
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C^{0pt}\rrbracket\left(f\right)
and
\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C^{0pt}\rrbracket\left(g\right)
are non-decreasing (non-increasing, respectively) sequences in
0pt\in\mathbb{N}
and it holds that

\sup_{0pt\in\mathbb{N}}\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C^{0pt}\rrbracket\left(f\right)~{}={}~\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\inf_{0pt\in\mathbb{N}}\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C^{0pt}\rrbracket\left(f\right)~{}={}~\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)~.

It should now be clear that, when considering the convergence of
\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)
for a program
C
with loops, we are implicitly considering two limits, the one over
N
and the one over
0pt
. Unfortunately, when the two limits have different monotonic behaviors (i.e., for
\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket
, where the limit in
0pt
is the supremum but the limit in
N
is the infimum, and symmetrically for
\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket
) convergence cannot be guaranteed. Intuitively, this explains why the next result only holds for
\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N}
and
\overline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{l}\overline{\mathsf{p}}^{N}
.

Theorem 7.6 (Convergence – General Case).

Let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})
be Riemann-suitable.
Then for all
C\in\mathsf{pWhile}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{G})
and post-expectations
f\in\mathcal{F}\cap{\mathbb{E}_{meas}}
and
g\in\mathcal{F}\cap{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}_{meas}}
it holds that:

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq 1}\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{n}\llbracket C^{n}\rrbracket\left(f\right)~{}={}~\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(f\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(g\right)~{}={}~\inf_{n\geq 1}\overline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{l}\overline{\mathsf{p}}^{n}\llbracket C^{n}\rrbracket\left(g\right)~.

Note that
Theorem

7.6
ensures that when considering a Riemann suitable class, the L.H.S. of the inequality in
Corollary

5.6
converges to the exact conditional weakest pre-expectation
\mathsf{cwp}
.

8.
Effective Verification of
\mathsf{pWhile}
-Programs

In the previous sections, we have assumed that guards and arithmetical expressions in programs as well as the resulting (pre/post)-expectations are purely mathematical objects.
To enable
effective
, i.e., automated, verification we now define, inspired by
(
Batz et al., 2021
;
Schröer et al., 2023
)
, concrete syntaxes for these objects.

8.1.
A Formal Language of Expressions

Let
\mathit{LV}
be a countably infinite set of logical variables ranged over by
x,y,\ldots
, etc.
The sets
\mathsf{Terms}
and
\mathsf{Guards}
of (syntactic)
terms
and
guards
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{t}~\Coloneqq~q\in{\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}}~{}\mid{}~x\in\mathit{LV}~{}\mid{}~\mathsf{t}+\mathsf{t}~{}\mid{}~\mathsf{t}\mathbin{\dot{-}}\mathsf{t}~{}\mid{}~\mathsf{t}\cdot\mathsf{t}\qquad\qquad\upvarphi~\Coloneqq~\mathsf{t}<\mathsf{t}~{}\mid{}~\neg\upvarphi~{}\mid{}~\upvarphi\land\upvarphi

Note that the other standard comparison relations
\leq,\neq,=,>,\geq
and Boolean connectives
\lor,{}\rightarrow{},{}\leftrightarrow{}
can be expressed in terms of
<,\neg,\land
.
We allow further syntactic sugar such as
\mathsf{t}^{2}
for
\mathsf{t}\cdot\mathsf{t}
and
1\leq x\leq 2
for
1\leq x\land x\leq 2
, etc.
Additional parentheses are admitted to clarify the order of precedence; to minimize the use of parentheses we assume that
\cdot
takes precedence over
+
and
\mathbin{\dot{-}}
, and
\neg
binds stronger than the binary Boolean connectives, as is standard.
We let
{\mathsf{free}(\mathsf{t})}
and
{\mathsf{free}(\upvarphi)}
be the variables occurring in term
\mathsf{t}
and guard
\upvarphi
.

For every
{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}\supseteq{\mathsf{free}(\mathsf{t})}
, the semantics
\llbracket\mathsf{t}\rrbracket\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
of
\mathsf{t}\in\mathsf{Terms}
is standard except that
\mathbin{\dot{-}}
is interpreted as
“monus”
, i.e.,
\llbracket\mathsf{t}_{1}\mathbin{\dot{-}}\mathsf{t}_{2}\rrbracket=\max(\mathsf{t}_{1}-\mathsf{t}_{2},0)
.
The semantics of
\upvarphi\in\mathsf{Guards}
can be viewed similarly as a function
\llbracket\upvarphi\rrbracket\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{B}
for every
{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}\supseteq{\mathsf{free}(\upvarphi)}
.
For
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}
, we write
\sigma\models\upvarphi
and
\sigma\not\models\upvarphi
to indicate that
\llbracket\upvarphi\rrbracket(\sigma)=\mathsf{true}
and
\llbracket\upvarphi\rrbracket(\sigma)=\mathsf{false}
, respectively.

Note that every
\mathsf{t}\in\mathsf{Terms}
without monus is a non-negative polynomial in the — finitely many — variables
{\mathsf{free}(\mathsf{t})}
with rational coefficients, possibly written in (partially) factorized form, whereas terms with monus can be seen as a piecewise defined non-negative polynomial.
Similarly, every
\upvarphi\in\mathsf{Guards}
is a Boolean combination of (in)equations between such (piecewise) polynomials.

We are now ready to define our expression language similar to
(
Batz et al., 2021
)
.
The restriction to infima and suprema over
compact
intervals
{[a,b]}
resembles the definition of lower and upper Riemann sums.

Definition 8.1 (The Expression Language
\mathsf{Expr}
).

The set of
\mathsf{Expr}
of (syntactic)
expressions
is defined according to the following grammar:

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}

\displaystyle\quad\Coloneqq\quad\mathsf{t}~{}\mid{}~{\left[\upvarphi\right]}\cdot\mathsf{f}~{}\mid{}~q\cdot\mathsf{f}~{}\mid{}~\mathsf{f}+\mathsf{f}~{}\mid{}~{\reflectbox{{{S}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\!_{[a,b]}\,x\colon}\,\mathsf{f}~{}\mid{}~{\raisebox{0.6pt}{\rotatebox{-30.0}{{{\reflectbox{J}}}}\hskip-0.43057pt}\!_{[a,b]}\,x\colon}\,\mathsf{f}

Here,
\mathsf{t}\in\mathsf{Terms}
,
\upvarphi\in\mathsf{Guards}
,
q\in{{\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}}}
,
a,b\in{{\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}}}
,
a\leq b
, and
x\in\mathit{LV}
.

\triangle

We allow
{\left[\upvarphi\right]}
as syntactic sugar for
{\left[\upvarphi\right]}\cdot 1
and adopt the usual rules regarding parentheses and orders of precedence.
For
\mathsf{f}\in\mathsf{Expr}
we let
{\mathsf{free}(\mathsf{f})}
be the variables in
\mathsf{f}
that are not bound by a

S

(supremum) or

J

(infimum)
“quantifier”
.
As in standard first-order logic, it is possible for a variable to have both a free and a non-free occurrence in
\mathsf{f}
.
We may write
\mathsf{f}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
to indicate that
\mathsf{f}
contains at most the pairwise distinct free variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\mathit{LV}
.

Definition 8.2 (Semantics of Expressions).

We define the semantics of expressions
\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
for every finite
{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathit{LV}
with
{\mathsf{free}(\mathsf{f})}\subseteq{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}
inductively as follows.
For all
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}
:

•

\llbracket\mathsf{t}\rrbracket(\sigma)
is defined in the standard manner, see above.

•

\llbracket{\left[\upvarphi\right]}\cdot\mathsf{f}\rrbracket(\sigma)=\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket(\sigma)
if
\sigma\models\upvarphi
;
\llbracket{\left[\upvarphi\right]}\cdot\mathsf{f}\rrbracket(\sigma)=0
if
\sigma\not\models\upvarphi
.

•

\llbracket q\cdot\mathsf{f}\rrbracket(\sigma)=q\cdot\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket(\sigma)
.

•

\llbracket\mathsf{f}_{1}+\mathsf{f}_{2}\rrbracket(\sigma)=\llbracket\mathsf{f}_{1}\rrbracket(\sigma)+\llbracket\mathsf{f}_{2}\rrbracket(\sigma)

•

\llbracket{\reflectbox{{{S}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\!_{[a,b]}\,x\colon}\,\mathsf{f}\rrbracket(\sigma)=\sup_{\xi\in{[a,b]}}\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket(\sigma[x\mapsto\xi])
.

•

\llbracket{\raisebox{0.6pt}{\rotatebox{-30.0}{{{\reflectbox{J}}}}\hskip-0.43057pt}\!_{[a,b]}\,x\colon}\,\mathsf{f}\rrbracket(\sigma)=\inf_{\xi\in{[a,b]}}\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket(\sigma[x\mapsto\xi])
.

In the last two cases, if
x
is not already in the domain of
\sigma
, we tacitly assume that the operation
\sigma[x\mapsto\xi]
extends the domain of
\sigma
by
x
.

\triangle

It is not immediately clear that
\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket
is well-defined due to the suprema in
Definition

8.2
— we have to ensure that those exist in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
We show this now.

Lemma 8.3.

For every
\mathsf{f}\in\mathsf{Expr}
and every finite
{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathit{LV}
with
{\mathsf{free}(\mathsf{f})}\subseteq{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}
the semantics
\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a well-defined locally bounded function (cf.
Section

7.1
).

Example 8.4.

\mathsf{f}={\reflectbox{{{S}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\!_{[0,5]}\,x\colon}{\left[w=x^{2}\right]}\cdot x
denotes the function
\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket=\lambda\sigma.{\left[0\leq\sigma(w)\leq 25\right]}\cdot\sqrt{\sigma(w)}
.

8.2.
Properties of Syntactic Expressions

The set
\mathsf{FO}
of first-order formulae
12
12

12

In this paper,
first-order formula
always refers to first-order logic over the signature of polynomial arithmetic with comparison; we interpret such formulae over the fixed structure
\mathbb{R}
.
is defined according to the following grammar:

\displaystyle\uppsi\quad\Coloneqq\quad\upvarphi~{}\mid{}~\exists x\colon\uppsi~{}\mid{}~\forall x\colon\uppsi~{}\mid{}~\neg\uppsi~{}\mid{}~\uppsi\land\uppsi~{}\mid{}~\uppsi\lor\uppsi~{}\mid{}~\uppsi{}\rightarrow{}\uppsi

where
\upvarphi
is a Boolean combination of polynomial (in)equalities (similar to the guards defined in
Section

8.1
but with standard minus instead of
“monus”
), and
x\in\mathit{LV}
.
As usual,
{\mathsf{free}(\uppsi)}
is the set of free variables in
\uppsi
, and we write
\uppsi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
to indicate that
\uppsi
contains at most the (pairwise distinct) free variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\mathit{LV}
.
An FO formula
\uppsi
is called
quantifier-free
if it does not contain any
\exists
and
\forall
quantifiers.
Moreover,
\uppsi
is said to be in
prenex normal form
(PNF) if
\uppsi=\reflectbox{$\mathsf{Q}$}\hskip 0.86108pt_{1}x_{1}\colon\ldots\reflectbox{$\mathsf{Q}$}\hskip 0.86108pt_{n}x_{n}\colon\uppsi^{\prime}
where
n\geq 0
,
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{Q}$}\hskip 0.86108pt_{1},\ldots,\reflectbox{$\mathsf{Q}$}\hskip 0.86108pt_{n}\in\{\exists,\forall\}
, and
\uppsi^{\prime}
is quantifier-free.
Similarly,
\uppsi
is in
existential (universal) prenex form
if for all
1\leq i\leq n
we have
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{Q}$}\hskip 0.86108pt_{i}=\exists
(
\reflectbox{$\mathsf{Q}$}\hskip 0.86108pt_{i}=\forall
, respectively).

The semantics of a formula
\uppsi\in\mathsf{FO}
is standard and can be viewed as a function
\llbracket\uppsi\rrbracket\colon\mathbb{R}^{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{B}
for all
{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathit{LV}
with
{\mathsf{free}(\uppsi)}\subseteq{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}
.
For
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}}
we write
\sigma\models\uppsi
to indicate that
\sigma
is a model of
\uppsi
, i.e., the
sentence
(formula without free variables) obtained by substituting every free occurrence of
x\in{\mathsf{free}(\uppsi)}
in
\uppsi
by
\sigma(x)
evaluates to
\mathsf{true}
in
\mathbb{R}
.
A formula
\uppsi
is called
satisfiable
if it has a model,
unsatisfiable
if it does not have a model, and
valid
if
\neg\uppsi
is unsatisfiable.

The decision problem of checking whether a given
quantifier-free

\uppsi\in\mathsf{FO}
is satisfiable is called
\mathsf{QF\_NRA}
13
13

13

Quantifier-free non-linear real arithmetic, a term coined by the SMT-community
(
Barrett et al., 2016
)
.
.
It is known that
\mathsf{QF\_NRA}\in\mathsf{PSPACE}
.
Checking the satisfiability of general
\mathsf{FO}
formulae is decidable as well, but has higher complexity.

Lemma 8.5 (
\mathsf{Expr}
to
\mathsf{FO}
).

For every
\mathsf{f}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\mathsf{Expr}
there exists an FO formula
\uppsi_{\mathsf{f}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y)
encoding
\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket
in the sense that for all
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathsf{free}(\mathsf{f})}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we have that
\sigma,r\models\uppsi_{\mathsf{f}}
iff
\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket(\sigma)=r
.
For quantifier-free
\mathsf{f}
, we can construct
\uppsi_{\mathsf{f}}
in existential prenex form in linear time.

FO-expressible functions in the sense of
Lemma

8.5
have been extensively studied
(
Coste, 2000
)
.
Let
\mathsf{FO}_{\mathbb{R}}
be defined like
\mathsf{FO}
with the only difference that
arbitrary real numbers
are allowed as coefficients in the polynomials.
A set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
n\geq 1
, is called
semi-algebraic
if there exists an
\mathsf{FO}_{\mathbb{R}}
formula
\uppsi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
such that
A=\{\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid\sigma\models\uppsi\}
.
A function
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is called semi-algebraic if its graph
\{(\sigma,f(\sigma))\mid\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\}
is a semi-algebraic set.

Lemma 8.6 (
e.g.,
(
Coste, 2000
, Exercise 2.22)
).

Every semi-algebraic function
f\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
has at most finitely many discontinuities.
In particular,
f
is almost everywhere continuous and hence Riemann-integrable on every interval
{[a,b]}\subset\mathbb{R}
where
f
is bounded.

By combining local boundedness from
Lemma

8.3
, the translation to FO from
Lemma

8.5
, and the fact that semi-algebraic functions are Riemann-integrable (
Lemma

8.6
), we obtain:

Theorem 8.7.

For all
\mathsf{f}\in\mathsf{Expr}
, intervals
{[a,b]}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, finite
{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathit{LV}
with
{\mathsf{free}(\mathsf{f})}\subseteq{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}
, and variables
x\in{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}
, the function
\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\mathit{LV}^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is Riemann-integrable on
{[a,b]}
w.r.t.
x
.

In analogy to standard FO, we say that an expression
\mathsf{f}\in\mathsf{Expr}
is in
prenex normal form
(PNF) if

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}~{}={}~{\reflectbox{{{Q}}}\hskip 0.86108pt^{\!1}\!_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\,x_{1}\colon}\ldots{\reflectbox{{{Q}}}\hskip 0.86108pt^{\!n}\!_{[a_{n},b_{n}]}\,x_{n}\colon}\mathsf{f}^{\prime}

where
n\geq 0
,
\reflectbox{{{Q}}}\hskip 0.86108pt^{\!1},\ldots,\reflectbox{{{Q}}}\hskip 0.86108pt^{\!n}\in\{\raisebox{0.6pt}{\rotatebox{-30.0}{{{\reflectbox{J}}}}\hskip-0.43057pt},\reflectbox{{{S}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\}
, and
\mathsf{f}^{\prime}
is quantifier-free.

Lemma 8.8 (Prenex Normal Form

(
Batz et al., 2021
, Lemma 8.1)
).

Every
\mathsf{f}\in\mathsf{Expr}
can be transformed into an equivalent
\mathsf{f}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Expr}
in PNF with the same free variables.
Moreover, if
\mathsf{f}
is

J

-free (

S

-free), then
\mathsf{f}^{\prime}
is

J

-free (

S

-free, respectively) as well.
This transformation can be done in linear time.

Next, we formalize one of our key insights:
In order to check a
“
quantitative entailment
”

\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket\mathsf{g}\rrbracket
such that suprema occur only in
\mathsf{f}
and infima only in
\mathsf{g}
, it is
not
actually necessary to evaluate them exactly (which amounts to solving optimization problems).
Instead, one can, loosely speaking, replace
\sup
and
\inf
by
\forall
-quantifiers.
This idea is based on the following elementary observation:
For arbitrary sets
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, we have
\sup A\leq\inf B
iff
\forall a\in A,b\in B\colon~a\leq b
.

Lemma 8.9 (Checking Quantitative Entailments
, cf.
(
Schröer et al., 2023
, Section 5.2)
).

Let

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}~{}={}~{\reflectbox{{{S}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\!_{[a_{1},b_{1}]}\,x_{1}\colon}\ldots{\reflectbox{{{S}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\!_{[a_{n},b_{n}]}\,x_{n}\colon}\mathsf{f}^{\prime}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{g}~{}={}~{\raisebox{0.6pt}{\rotatebox{-30.0}{{{\reflectbox{J}}}}\hskip-0.43057pt}\!_{[c_{1},d_{1}]}\,z_{1}\colon}\ldots{\raisebox{0.6pt}{\rotatebox{-30.0}{{{\reflectbox{J}}}}\hskip-0.43057pt}\!_{[c_{m},d_{m}]}\,z_{m}\colon}\mathsf{g}^{\prime}

be

J

-free (

S

-free, respectively) expressions in PNF.
Further, let
\uppsi_{\mathsf{f}^{\prime}}({\mathsf{free}(\mathsf{f}^{\prime})},y_{\mathsf{f}})
and
\uppsi_{\mathsf{g}^{\prime}}({\mathsf{free}(\mathsf{g}^{\prime})},y_{\mathsf{g}})
be the FO formulae in existential prenex form encoding
\mathsf{f}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{g}^{\prime}
from
Lemma

8.5
.
Then:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket~{}\sqsubseteq{}~\llbracket\mathsf{g}\rrbracket

(4)

iff

\displaystyle\Bigg(\bigwedge_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\,\in\,{\mathsf{free}(\mathsf{f})}\\
\cup\,{\mathsf{free}(\mathsf{g})}\end{subarray}}x{\geq}0~\land~\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}{\leq}x_{i}{\leq}b_{i}~\land~\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}c_{i}{\leq}z_{i}{\leq}d_{i}~\land~\uppsi_{\mathsf{f}^{\prime}}\land\uppsi_{\mathsf{g}^{\prime}}\Bigg)~{}\rightarrow{}~y_{\mathsf{f}}\leq y_{\mathsf{g}}\quad\text{is valid}

As a consequence, the decision problem
“
\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket\mathsf{g}\rrbracket
?”
for
\mathsf{f}
and
\mathsf{g}
is linear-time reducible to
\mathsf{QF\_NRA}
.

Example 8.10.

Reconsider the expression
\mathsf{f}={\reflectbox{{{S}}}\hskip 0.86108pt\!_{[0,5]}\,x\colon}{\left[w=x^{2}\right]}\cdot x
from
Example

8.4
.
Suppose we wish to check whether
\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket
is upper-bounded by the constant function
\llbracket\mathsf{g}\rrbracket
,
\mathsf{g}=4
.
Since
\mathsf{f}
and
\mathsf{g}
have the form required by
Lemma

8.9
we can achieve this by considering the FO formula

\displaystyle\left(w\geq 0\land 0\leq x\leq 5\land(w=x^{2}{}\rightarrow{}y=x)\land(w\neq x^{2}{}\rightarrow{}y=0)\land y^{\prime}=4\right)~{}\rightarrow{}~y\leq y^{\prime}

which is
not
valid as witnessed by the assignment
\{x\mapsto 5,w\mapsto 25,y\mapsto 5,y^{\prime}\mapsto 4\}
.
Hence
\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\not\sqsubseteq 4
.

8.3.
Decidability and Complexity of
\mathsf{pWhile}
Verification

We now assume (w.l.o.g.) that the set of logical variables
\mathit{LV}
used in syntactic expressions contains our fixed set
V
of program variables.

Definition 8.11 (Representable and Syntactic Expectations).

We define the following terminology:

•

An expectation
f\in\mathbb{E}
is called
representable
if
f=\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket
for some
\mathsf{f}\in\mathsf{Expr}
with
{\mathsf{free}(\mathsf{f})}\subseteq V
.
The set of all representable (1-bounded) expectations is denoted
\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{Expr}}
(
\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}_{\mathsf{Expr}}
, respectively).

•

A
syntactic expectation
is an expression
\mathsf{f}\in\mathsf{Expr}
whose free variables are program variables, i.e.,
{\mathsf{free}(\mathsf{f})}\subseteq V
.
A
1-bounded
syntactic expectation
\mathsf{Expr}^{\leq 1}
is
\mathsf{f}\in\mathsf{Expr}
such that
\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\in{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}}
.
The set of all 1-bounded syntactic expectations is denoted
\mathsf{Expr}^{\leq 1}
.

\triangle

Membership in
\mathsf{Expr}^{\leq 1}
is decidable by encoding
\mathsf{f}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
as the FO formula
\uppsi_{\mathsf{f}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y)
from
Lemma

8.5
and checking validity of
(x_{1}\geq 0\land\ldots\land x_{n}\geq 0\land\uppsi_{\mathsf{f}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y)){}\rightarrow{}y\leq 1
.

Our next theorem implies that if we restrict our framework to representable expectations and only allow the
\mathsf{Terms}
and
\mathsf{Guards}
defined in
Section

8.1
as the arithmetic and Boolean, respectively, expressions in our programs, then we obtain the convergence guarantees from
Section

7
.

Theorem 8.12 (Riemann-suitability of
\mathsf{Expr}
).

The combination
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{Expr}},\mathsf{Terms},\mathsf{Guards})
with
\mathsf{Terms}
and
\mathsf{Guards}
as defined in
Section

8.1
is Riemann-suitable (see
Definition

7.3
).

8.3.1.
Verifying Loop-free Programs

Since
\mathsf{Expr}
is effectively closed under weakest (liberal) Riemann pre-expectation (formalized in
Section

C.20
), it follows that we can effectively prove upper and lower bounds on weakest (liberal) pre-expectations of loop-free programs:

Theorem 8.13 (Bounds on Loop-free
\mathsf{w}(\mathsf{l})\mathsf{p}
).

For all loop-free
C\in\mathsf{pWhile}(\mathsf{Terms},\mathsf{Guards})
and integers
N\geq 1
the following decision problems can be effectively translated
14
14

14

In polynomial time if we assume
C
to be of constant size and
N
given in unary. In general, an expression encoding
\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\right)
for a given
\mathsf{f}
can be exponential in the size of
C
— consider
n
sequential
\mathtt{if}
-
\mathtt{else}
constructs. However, our reduction is of practical interest as
\mathsf{QF\_NRA}
is supported by many SMT solvers.

to
\mathsf{QF\_NRA}
:
Given …

(1)

… an

S

-free
\mathsf{f}\in\mathsf{Expr}
and an

J

-free
\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{Expr}
, is
\llbracket\mathsf{g}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\right)
?

If yes, then
\llbracket\mathsf{g}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\right)
, provided
\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\in{\mathbb{E}_{meas}}
.

(2)

… an

J

-free
\mathsf{f}\in\mathsf{Expr}
and an

S

-free
\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{Expr}
, is
\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\right)\sqsubseteq\llbracket\mathsf{g}\rrbracket
?

If yes, then
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\right)\sqsubseteq\llbracket\mathsf{g}\rrbracket
, provided
\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\in{\mathbb{E}_{meas}}
.

(3)

… an

S

-free
\mathsf{f}\in\mathsf{Expr}^{\leq 1}
and an

J

-free
\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{Expr}^{\leq 1}
, is
\llbracket\mathsf{g}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\right)
?

If yes, then
\llbracket\mathsf{g}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\right)
), provided
\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\in{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}_{meas}}
.

(4)

… an

J

-free
\mathsf{f}\in\mathsf{Expr}^{\leq 1}
and an

S

-free
\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{Expr}^{\leq 1}
, is
\overline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{l}\overline{\mathsf{p}}^{N}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\right)\sqsubseteq\llbracket\mathsf{g}\rrbracket
?

If yes, then
\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\right)\sqsubseteq\llbracket\mathsf{g}\rrbracket
, provided
\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\in{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}_{meas}}
.

Moreover, for fixed
C
and
\mathsf{f}
, the size of the syntactic expectation representing
\mathcal{T}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\right)
for
\mathcal{T}\in\{\underline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{p}^{N},\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N},\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N},\overline{\mathsf{w}}\mathsf{l}\overline{\mathsf{p}}^{N}\}
is in
\mathcal{O}\left(N^{k}\right)
, where
k
is the number of
\mathtt{unif}
-statements
in
C
.

Using
Theorem

8.13
and
Corollary

5.6
, it is straightforward to derive bounds on
\mathsf{cwp}
as well.

8.3.2.
Verifying Loops

We now state how to apply the proof rules from
Section

6
effectively:

Theorem 8.14 (Verification of Invariants).

Let
C=\mathtt{while}~(\varphi)~\{B\}\in\mathsf{pWhile}(\mathsf{Terms},\mathsf{Guards})
such that
B
is loop-free.
Further, let
\mathsf{I},\mathsf{f}\in\mathsf{Expr}
and
\mathsf{J},\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{Expr}^{\leq 1}
all be quantifier-free, and let
N\geq 1
be an integer.
Then we can …

(1)

… decide if
\llbracket\mathsf{I}\rrbracket
is a
\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}
-superinvariant of
C
w.r.t.
\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket
by a reduction to
\mathsf{QF\_NRA}
.

If the superinvariant property holds, then
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\right)\sqsubseteq\llbracket I\rrbracket
, provided
\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\in{\mathbb{E}_{meas}}

(2)

… decide if
\llbracket\mathsf{J}\rrbracket
is a
\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{N}
-subinvariant of
C
w.r.t.
\llbracket\mathsf{g}\rrbracket
by a reduction to
\mathsf{QF\_NRA}
.

If the subinvariant property holds, then
\llbracket J\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\llbracket\mathsf{g}\rrbracket\right)
, provided
\llbracket\mathsf{g}\rrbracket\in{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}_{meas}}
.

Theorem 8.15 (Complexity of Verification Problems).

The following decision problems are
\mathsf{coRE}
-complete
15
15

15

I.e.,
\Pi_{1}^{0}
-complete in the arithmetical hierarchy.
:
Given an arbitrary (not necessarily loop-free)
C\in\mathsf{pWhile}(\mathsf{Terms},\mathsf{Guards})
and …

(1)

…
\mathsf{f},\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{Expr}
such that
\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\in{\mathbb{E}_{meas}}
, does it hold that
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\right)\sqsubseteq\llbracket\mathsf{g}\rrbracket
?

(2)

…
\mathsf{f},\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{Expr}^{\leq 1}
such that
\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\in{\mathbb{E}^{\leq 1}_{meas}}
, does it hold that
\llbracket\mathsf{g}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\right)
?

The proof of membership in
\mathsf{coRE}
heavily relies on the convergence results established in
Theorem

7.6
.
The
\mathsf{coRE}
-hardness proofs are standard
(
Kaminski and Katoen, 2015
)
and follow from
\mathsf{coRE}
-hardness of the non-halting problem.
See
Section

C.23
for details.

9.
Implementation and Case Studies

We have automated our techniques using a modern deductive verifier for probabilistic programs. We first describe how to integrate our techniques within that verifier in
Section

9.1
. We then describe our case studies, i.e., programs and specifications we have verified, in
Section

9.2
. Finally, we evaluate our approach empirically on these case studies in
Section

9.3
.

9.1.
Implementation

Caesar

16
16

16

https://www.caesarverifier.org/

(
Schröer et al., 2023
)
is a recent expectation-based automated deductive verifier targeting
discrete
and possibly
nondeterministic
17
17

17

Here we refer to pure nondeterminism which is to be resolved angelically or demonically.
probabilistic programs. As input,
Caesar
takes programs written in an intermediate verification language called
\mathsf{HeyVL}
— a quantitative analogue of
Boogie

(
Leino, 2008
)
.
\mathsf{HeyVL}
provides a programmatic means to describe verification conditions such as the validity of quantitative loop invariants. These verification conditions are offloaded to an SMT solver, which enables the semi-automated verification of said discrete probabilistic programs.

Caesar
does
not
support continuous sampling instructions. We will, however, now demonstrate that with our approach based on Riemann sums,
Caesar

can
readily be used to verify bounds on expected outcomes of continuous probabilistic programs in a semi-automated fashion.

Our key insight is that our Riemann expectation transformers can be expressed as expectation transformers denoted by
discrete
probabilistic programs featuring angelic (resp. demonic)
nondeterministic choices
for
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
-valued intervals. The latter features
are
supported by
Caesar
:
\mathsf{HeyVL}
supports the discrete fragment of
\mathsf{pWhile}
and, amongst others, the two statements

(1)

\mathtt{x}\coloneq[E_{1},E_{2}]
, which, on program state
\sigma
, assigns a nondeterministically chosen value from the
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
-valued interval
[E_{1}(\sigma),E_{2}(\sigma)]
to the variable
\mathtt{x}
, and

(2)

\mathtt{x}:\approx\mathtt{discrete\_unif}\left(N\right)
, which, given a (constant) natural number
N\geq 1
, samples a value from the set
\{0,\ldots,N-1\}
uniformly at random and assigns the result to the variable
\mathtt{x}
.

Notice that the latter statement is syntactic sugar for
\mathsf{pWhile}
as it can be simulated by binary probabilistic choices. The statement
\mathtt{x}\coloneq[E_{1},E_{2}]
, on the other hand, is not syntactic sugar as nondeterminism is not supported in
\mathsf{pWhile}
.

Now, given a (discrete but possibly nondeterministic) program
C
,
Caesar
employs a transformer
\mathsf{vc}\llbracket C\rrbracket\colon\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{E}
, which is defined
18
18

18

We do not need to require
f
to be measurable since
C
is does not contain continuous sampling.
Moreover, as is standard for deductive verifiers, loops need to be annotated with quantitative loop invariants; see
Example

9.1
on page
9.1
.
as in
Table

1
and where for the additional statements, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{vc}\llbracket\mathtt{x}\coloneq[E_{1},E_{2}]\rrbracket\left(f\right)~{}={}~

\displaystyle\lambda\sigma.\sup_{\xi\in[E_{1}(\sigma),E_{2}(\sigma)]}f(\sigma[x\mapsto\xi])

\displaystyle\mathsf{vc}\llbracket\mathtt{x}:\approx\mathtt{discrete\_unif}\left(N\right)\rrbracket\left(f\right)~{}={}~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\cdot\sum\limits_{i=0}^{N-1}f[\mathtt{x}/i]~.

Notice that
\mathsf{vc}\llbracket\mathtt{x}\coloneq[E_{1},E_{2}]\rrbracket\left(f\right)
resolves the nondeterministic choice of
\mathtt{x}

angelically
by returning the
supremum
of all values obtained from evaluating
f
at
\sigma
where
\mathtt{x}
is set to some value from
[E_{1}(\sigma),E_{2}(\sigma)]
. The demonic counterpart is obtained from replacing
\sup
by
\inf
.
Now let
\mathtt{j}
be a fresh program variable and observe that for all
N\geq 1
and all
f\in\mathbb{E}
, we have

\underbrace{\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{N}\llbracket\mathtt{x}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}\rrbracket\left(f\right)}_{\text{defined in this paper}}~{}={}~\underbrace{\mathsf{vc}\llbracket\mathtt{j}:\approx\mathtt{discrete\_unif}\left(N\right)\,;\,\mathtt{x}\coloneq[\tfrac{\mathtt{j}}{N},\tfrac{\mathtt{j}+1}{N}]\rrbracket\left(f\right)}_{\text{expressible in $\mathsf{HeyVL}$ and thus supported by {{Caesar}} }}~.

Hence, the upper Riemann pre-expectation of
\mathtt{x}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}
w.r.t.
f
corresponds to the maximal — under all possible resolutions of the nondeterminism — expected final value of
f
obtained from (i) sampling one of the
N
intervals occurring in the Riemann sums uniformly at random and (ii) assigning to
\mathtt{x}
a nondeterministically chosen value from that sampled interval.
Lower
Riemann pre-expectations can be expressed analogously by resolving the nondeterminism
demonically
.

Example 9.1.

Reconsider the Monte Carlo
\pi
-approximator
C
and the superinvariant
I
of
C
w.r.t.
\mathtt{count}
from
Example

6.3
. We can provide
Caesar
with the following annotated loop:

\displaystyle\mathtt{@invariant}\left(\mathtt{count}+{\left[\mathtt{i}\leq\mathtt{M}\right]}\cdot\big(0.85\cdot((\mathtt{M}\mathbin{\dot{-}}\mathtt{i})+1)\big)\right)

\displaystyle\mathtt{while}~(\mathtt{i}\leq\mathtt{M})~\{

\displaystyle\qquad\mathtt{j}_{1}:\approx\mathtt{discrete\_unif}\left(16\right)\,;\,\mathtt{x}\coloneq[\tfrac{\mathtt{j}_{1}}{16},\tfrac{\mathtt{j}_{1}+1}{16}]\,;

\displaystyle\qquad\mathtt{j}_{2}:\approx\mathtt{discrete\_unif}\left(16\right)\,;\,\mathtt{y}\coloneq[\tfrac{\mathtt{j}_{2}}{16},\tfrac{\mathtt{j}_{2}+1}{16}]\,;

\displaystyle\qquad\mathtt{if}(\mathtt{x}^{2}+\mathtt{y}^{2}\leq 1)~\{\mathtt{count}\coloneq\mathtt{count}+1\}~\mathtt{else}~\{\mathtt{skip}\}\,;\,\mathtt{i}\coloneq\mathtt{i}+1\quad\}

We can then instruct
Caesar
to check whether
I
is a superinvariant of this loop w.r.t.
\mathtt{count}
. Since the loop body encodes appropriate upper Riemann pre-expectations,
Caesar
offloads the quantitative entailments corresponding to
Theorem

8.14
to an SMT solver to check them automatically.
For lower Riemann pre-expectations and subinvariants (for
\mathsf{wlp}
),
Caesar
can be used analogously.

9.2.
Case Studies

In what follows, we describe the programs and specifications we have verified using
Caesar
with the SMT solver
Z3

(
de Moura and Bjørner, 2008
)
as back-end. All case studies (including
Example

9.1
) have been verified within
12
seconds on an Apple M2. The precise inputs to
Caesar
are provided in
Section

C.24
. Both the verified bounds and the required parition sizes
N
where determined manually by guessing the respective weakest pre-expectations and increasing
N
until
Caesar
reports success.

9.2.1.
The Irwin-Hall Distribution

\displaystyle\mathtt{while}~(\mathtt{i}\leq\mathtt{M})~\{

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\mathtt{y}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}\,;\,\mathtt{x}\coloneq\mathtt{x}+\mathtt{y}\,;

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\mathtt{i}\coloneq\mathtt{i}+1\hskip 9.24994pt\}

\displaystyle\mathtt{while}~(\mathtt{i}\leq\mathtt{M})~\{

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\mathtt{y}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}\,;\,\mathtt{observe}(\mathtt{y}\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})\,;\,\mathtt{x}\coloneq\mathtt{x}+\mathtt{y}\,;

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\mathtt{i}\coloneq\mathtt{i}+1\hskip 9.24994pt\}

Figure 2.
Generating the standard Irwin-Hall distribution (left) and a variant with conditioning (right).

The Irwin-Hall distribution (parameterized in
\mathtt{M}
)
(
Johnson et al., 1995
)
is the sum of
\mathtt{M}
independent and identically distributed random variables, each of which is distributed uniformly over
[0,1]
. The loop
C
depicted in
Figure

2
(left) models this family of distributions: On each iteration,
C
samples a value for
\mathtt{y}
uniformly at random and adds the result to the variable
\mathtt{x}
. Hence, if initially
\mathtt{i}=1
,
\mathtt{x}=0
, and
\mathtt{M}\in\mathbb{N}
, then the final distribution of
\mathtt{x}
indeed corresponds to the Irwin-Hall distribution with parameter
\mathtt{M}
.

We now aim to upper-bound the expected final value of
\mathtt{x}
, i.e., the expectation of the Irwin-Hall distribution, for
arbitrary
values of
\mathtt{M}
. Towards this end, we employ our encoding from
Section

9.1
and use
Caesar
to automatically verify that the expectation

I=\mathtt{x}+{\left[\mathtt{i}\leq\mathtt{M}\right]}\cdot\big(1.1\cdot\tfrac{(\mathtt{M}\mathbin{\dot{-}}\mathtt{i})+1}{2}\big)

is a
\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{10}
-superinvariant of
C
w.r.t.
\mathtt{x}
. Hence, by
Theorem

6.2
, we get
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{x}\right)\sqsubseteq I
and thus

\text{for initial $\sigma$ with $\sigma(\mathtt{i})=1$, $\sigma(\mathtt{x})=0$, $\sigma(\mathtt{M})\in\mathbb{N}$ with $\sigma(\mathtt{M})\geq 1$:}~\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{x}\right)(\sigma)\leq 1.1\cdot\frac{\sigma(\mathtt{M})}{2}~.

Now consider a variant of the Irwin-Hall distribution where we condition each of the
\mathtt{M}
random variables to take a value in
[0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}]
.
This situation is modeled by the loop
C
depicted in
Figure

2
(right), where we employ conditioning inside of the loop. We aim to upper-bound
\mathsf{cwp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{x}\right)
, i.e., the
conditional expected final value of
\mathtt{x}
.
For that, we use
Caesar
to verify that the following expectation is a
\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{19}
-superinvariant of
C
w.r.t.
\mathtt{count}
(resp.
\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{2}
-subinvariant of
C
w.r.t.
1
):

\displaystyle I~{}={}~\mathtt{x}+{\left[\mathtt{i}\leq\mathtt{M}\right]}\cdot\big(1.5\cdot\frac{(\mathtt{M}\mathbin{\dot{-}}\mathtt{i})+1}{8}\big)\qquad\text{and}\qquad J~{}={}~{\left[\mathtt{i}\leq\mathtt{M}\right]}\cdot 0.5^{(\mathtt{M}\mathbin{\dot{-}}\mathtt{i})+1}+{\left[\mathtt{i}>\mathtt{M}\right]}\cdot 1~.

Hence, we get by
Theorem

6.2
that
\mathsf{cwp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{x}\right)(\sigma)
is well-defined for all
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{V}
and

\text{for initial $\sigma$ with $\sigma(\mathtt{i})=1$, $\sigma(\mathtt{x})=0$, $\sigma(\mathtt{M})\in\mathbb{N}$ with $\sigma(\mathtt{M})\geq 1$:}~\mathsf{cwp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{x}\right)(\sigma)\leq\frac{1.5\cdot\sigma(\mathtt{M})}{8\cdot 0.5^{\sigma(\mathtt{M})}}~.

Notice that, even though exponential functions like
0.5^{\mathtt{M}}
are not supported by our decidable language of syntactic expectations from
Section

8
, we can use
Caesar
to reason about it by means of standard user-defined domain declaration for exponentials (see
(
Schröer et al., 2023
, Section 5.1)
for details). Decidability of the corresponding entailments involving such domains is, however, not guaranteed.

9.2.2.
Reasoning about Diverging Programs

\displaystyle\mathtt{while}~(\mathtt{x}>0)~\{

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\mathtt{y}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}\,;\,\mathtt{y}\coloneq(\mathtt{b}\mathbin{\dot{-}}\mathtt{a})\cdot\mathtt{y}+\mathtt{a}\,;

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\mathtt{if}(\mathtt{y}\leq(\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b})/2)~\{\mathtt{diverge}\}~\mathtt{else}~\{\mathtt{skip}\}\,;

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\mathtt{x}\coloneq\mathtt{x}\mathbin{\dot{-}}1\hskip 9.24994pt\}

\displaystyle\mathtt{while}~(\mathtt{h}\leq\mathtt{t})~\{

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\{\mathtt{x}:\approx\mathtt{unif}_{{{[0,1]}}}\,;\,\mathtt{x}\coloneq 10\cdot\mathtt{x}\,;\,\mathtt{h}\coloneq\mathtt{h}+\mathtt{x}\}~[\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}]~\{\mathtt{skip}\}\,;

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\mathtt{t}\coloneq\mathtt{t}+1\,;

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\mathtt{count}\coloneq\mathtt{count}+1\hskip 9.24994pt\}

Figure 3.
A probably diverging loop (left) and a race between tortoise and hare (right)
(
Chakarov and Sankaranarayanan, 2014
)
.

Weakest
liberal
pre-expectations enable us to lower-bound divergence probabilities of programs involving continuous sampling.
Consider the loop
C
depicted in
Figure

3
(left).
In each iteration, we sample uniformly from the interval
[\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}]
(notice that
\mathtt{a}
,
\mathtt{b}
are uninitialized variables and hence correspond to parameters) and assign the result to
\mathtt{y}
.
Whenever we sample a value in
[0,\tfrac{\mathtt{a}+\mathtt{b}}{2}]
, the program diverges.
Using
Caesar
, we verify that

I~{}={}~{\left[\mathtt{a}\leq\mathtt{b}\right]}\cdot(1\mathbin{\dot{-}}0.5^{\mathtt{x}})

is a
\underline{\mathsf{wl}}\mathsf{p}^{2}
-subinvariant of
C
w.r.t.
0
. Hence, we have
I~{}\sqsubseteq{}~\mathsf{wlp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(0\right)
by
Theorem

6.2
, i.e., whenever
\mathtt{a}\leq\mathtt{b}
, then
C
diverges on initial state
\sigma
with probability at least
1-0.5^{\sigma(\mathtt{x})}
.

9.2.3.
Race between Tortoise and Hare

The loop
C
depicted in
Figure

3
(right) is adapted from
(
Chakarov and Sankaranarayanan, 2014
)
and models a race between a tortoise (
\mathtt{t}
) and a hare (
\mathtt{h}
).
As long as the hare did not overtake the tortoise, the hare flips a fair coin to decide whether to move or not. If the hare decides to move, it samples a distance uniformly at random from
[0,10]
.
The tortoise always moves exactly one step.

We now upper-bound the expected final value of variable
\mathtt{count}
. Towards this end, we use
Caesar
to verify that the expectation

I=\mathtt{count}+{\left[\mathtt{h}\leq\mathtt{t}\right]}\cdot 3.012\cdot\big((\mathtt{t}\mathbin{\dot{-}}\mathtt{h})+2\big)

is a
\overline{\mathsf{wp}}^{16}
-superinvariant of
C
w.r.t.
\mathtt{count}
. Hence, we get
\mathsf{wp}\llbracket C\rrbracket\left(\mathtt{count}\right)\sqsubseteq I
by
Theorem

6.2
, i.e., if
C
is executed on an initial state
\sigma
with
\sigma(\mathtt{count})=0
and
\sigma(\mathtt{h})\leq\sigma(\mathtt{t})
, then the expected final value of
\mathtt{count}
is at most
3.012\cdot(\sigma(\mathtt{t})-\sigma(\mathtt{h})+2)
.
